- *(Done: The Ram at the Bottom of the Field, Negros Oriental, 2026-11-14.)* *Proposal: The Ram at the Bottom of the Field* (2026-11-14): a hill school in 2050 wants a hydraulic ram (a hydram, a pump with no motor that uses the water hammer of a falling drive flow to lift a small part of that flow much higher) to bring a spring up to its tanks, and the order was sized as if the ram delivered the spring's whole flow. Work out from the energy balance q = e Q h / H (delivery q, drive flow Q, drive fall h, lift H, efficiency e, taking e from a manufacturer's or a development agency's published table, not memory) what a plausible spring gives each day against the school's need, how much more a longer drive pipe and a lower ram site buy, and why most of the water runs to waste on purpose. *Shut It Like a Door on a Sleeping Child* already tells water hammer as a danger on a gravity main: here the surge is the engine, so keep the story on the delivery ratio, not on surge pressure. (`node overlap.mjs hydram* "hydraulic ram"`: unused on 2026-11-14.)
- *(Done: The Short Night on the Backup Battery, Saint Claude's hall (invented), Woonsocket, Rhode Island, Sat 18 to Sun 19 Sep 2049; Trojan 27-AGM rates 77/82/89/99 Ah at 5/10/20/100 h and 158 min at 25 A, interpolated log-log: 70.8 Ah at 20 A, 80.4 Ah at 10 A; 40% duty; 11.1 h promised, 8.84 h real (empty 06:01), two in parallel 20.1 h (2.27x); second battery at 00:30 gives about 16.3 h more; power back 11:40; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Proposal: The Short Night on the Backup Battery* (2026-11-14): a parish hall's cellar sump pump in 2049 runs on a lead-acid backup battery through a storm night when the mains is down, and the hall's assistant promised the warden "eleven hours" by dividing the label's amp-hours by the pump's current. The label's figure is a 20-hour rate; at the pump's much higher current the battery gives less, which is Peukert's law (t = H (C / (I H))^k). Work out before drafting, from a real manufacturer's discharge table for one named battery size (not a remembered exponent; lead-acid is usually quoted at k about 1.1 to 1.3, lithium iron phosphate close to 1), how many hours the pump really gets, what a duty cycle (the float switch running the pump in bursts) does to the answer, and what a second battery in parallel buys, which is more than double the hours. The plot turns on the hour the cellar starts to fill. Keep clear of the energy-budget shortfalls already told (*The Lamp That Is Dark Before Dawn*, *The Minibus That Is Twenty-Eight Short*) and of battery gassing (*The Cupboard That Breathes Out*): the subject here is the rate, not the total. (`node overlap.mjs Peukert "sump pump"`: both unused on 2026-11-14.)
- *(Done: The Last Quarter Metre, Llanidwal (invented), Carmarthenshire, Thu 16 to Fri 17 Aug 2063; tank 8 x 6 m, 2.50 m, 120 m³; 40 mm scour, Cd 0.6; test hour 2.50 to 2.12 m; straight line 6.57 h (empty 00:34), Torricelli 12.62 h (empty 06:37); half depth at 3.70 h, last 0.25 m takes 3.99 h; 120 m siphon to a field tank 18 m below at about 1.48 L/s from 22:30 brings 1 cm at 02:21 and gives 20.2 m³; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Proposal* (2026-11-15): Torricelli's law. A hill village's gravity-fed water tank (or a flooded church crypt being drained through one low pipe) empties through an outlet whose flow falls with the square root of the head, v = √(2gh) times a discharge coefficient of about 0.6, so the first hour's drop, extrapolated in a straight line by the village's assistant, promises the tank empty (or the crypt dry) far sooner than it will be: from full to half the depth takes only about 29% of the whole draining time, and the last tenth of the depth takes about 32%. Work out before drafting, for one named tank size and outlet diameter, the real time to empty against the straight-line forecast, and what a second outlet or a pump changes. The plot turns on a promise made from the first hour (a delivery, a Mass in the crypt, a repair that needs the tank dry). Keep clear of the energy-budget shortfalls and of water hammer (*The Ram at the Bottom of the Field*). (`node overlap.mjs Torricelli`: unused on 2026-11-15.)
- *(Done: Ten Tonnes Up, Saint Brigid's hall (invented), Drumlahan (invented), near Mohill, Co. Leitrim, Tue 22 to Thu 31 Jan 2058; text only.)* Buried 10 m³ polyethylene rainwater tank (2.3 m dia x 2.5 m, 450 kg, top 0.75 m down) in a pea-gravel surround in clay (the bathtub effect); uplift 10.39 t fully submerged vs 5.67 t holding down with the dipwell at 0.60 m (fill 1,835 kg/m³ dry, 1,040 buoyant, straight column, no side friction); balance with water 1.77 m down, 1.25 margin at 2.06 m (installer's 2041 letter said 2 m); gravel pumped through the 110 mm dipwell (5.88 m³, 82 min at 1.2 L/s); refilled overnight by 17 mm on the 320 m² hall roof (5.44 m³) via the tank overflow into a soakaway in the same gravel, so the winter reading measured the roof, not the ground; overflow bunged to the ditch, re-pumped (75 min), tanker 56 min, refill to 1.8 m (FS 1.27) at 20 L/min in 6.2 h; a plastic septic tank that floated at Cloone in 2036 as the cost.
- *(Done: The Water That Walks Up, Gudziszki (invented) near Sejny, Podlasie, Poland, Sat 2 Nov 2052 to Sun 2 Feb 2053; text only. Silt at 352 kg/m³ water, 51 L/m³ air takes the 9% in-place expansion (32 L); 550 °C·days × n 0.9, Stefan 1.11 m, ×0.85 = 0.95 m (mild 300: 0.70 m) vs 0.5 m pads and the 1.4 m PN-81/B-03020 zone; segregation potential 0.8e-3 mm²/(s·K) gives 75 mm heave at the wet corner, 37 mm below 0.5 m, dry corner ~12 mm; 25 mm over 1.6 m tilts 0.93°, 68 mm at 4.2 m; 12.3 kPa pad pressure; adfreeze 30-120 kN on a 0.3 m pier vs 2 kN; new footings 1.5 m, gravel backfill and polythene slip sleeve, moved 3 m off a filled 1971 pond; dilatancy test in the palm.)* Frost heave and a summer-dug footing. A parish or village builds something light on shallow footings late in the year (a lychgate, a bus shelter, a small bell frame), and the assistant checks the concrete and the load but not the ground: in a frost-susceptible silt with water near the surface, ice lenses grow at the freezing front by drawing water up, and a footing above the frost depth is lifted unevenly by centimetres, then dropped when it thaws. Work out before drafting, for one named site (soil, water table, a cold winter's freezing index), the frost penetration depth (a Stefan or modified Berggren estimate, stated as an estimate), how much a few ice lenses lift, why a coarse gravel does not heave, and what a sensible builder does (go below frost depth, insulate, or drain). The turn is a reasonable no from an older mason; the cost shown is something already cracked by heave. Keep clear of the tank that floats (*Ten Tonnes Up*), the land rising after the ice (*The Rising Shore*) and the ground under snow (*The Ground Under the Drift*; `node overlap.mjs "frost heave" "ice lens"`: unused on 2026-11-15).
- *(Done: What the Rain Carries, Tawhai Creek, South Canterbury, December 2058.)* *Proposal* (2026-11-15): copper runoff and the galvanised gutter. A parish re-roofs a sacristy in copper and lets its rain run onto galvanised iron below; copper in the runoff plates out on the zinc and pits it through. Work out the runoff (a published range), the gram-for-gram swap, and why the average loss is the wrong number for a pit; the fix is to separate the flows (`node overlap.mjs galvanic corrosion`: unused on 2026-11-15).
- *(Done: Joined at Ten Past Five, Valdecañas del Páramo (invented), Soria, Spain, July to November 2049, 2026-11-15; text only.)* Numbers used: 32 mm PE100 SDR11, 220 m, alpha 1.8e-4/K, 60 C at the joint to 9 C ground, 2.02 m free contraction; pressure end load 183 N vs thermal pull 0.97-1.95 kN; end slip 13-51 mm by an end-sliding model (story 37 mm, seal at 36 mm); snaking 0.39% = 0.87 m; leak 38.5 m3. Original proposal: thermal contraction of a long plastic pipe laid in the sun. Volunteers lay a polyethylene water pipe a couple of hundred metres from a village main to a cemetery tap, allotments or a summer camp, on a hot July afternoon with the black pipe lying in the sun all day, and connect both ends tight with compression fittings; the assistant checked the flow, the pressure rating and the trench depth, not the temperature of the pipe on the day it was joined. The first cold night the pipe shortens and pulls out of a fitting (or strains it until it lets go weeks later). Work out before drafting, for one named pipe (material, diameter, length, the pipe's own temperature in the sun against the ground's in autumn), how far it shortens (expansion coefficient about 1.5 to 2 × 10⁻⁴ per kelvin, roughly ten times steel's), the pull on a fitting if it is held (and why creep makes that an estimate), and what a sensible fitter does (snake it in the trench, let it cool to ground temperature before the last joint, anchor or use end-load-resistant fittings). The turn is an older plumber or farmer who has had it happen; the cost shown is water lost or a grave plot flooded before anyone noticed. Keep clear of the stories that already use frost, pipes that freeze or go flat, and water hammer (`node overlap.mjs "thermal expansion" "contraction" "MDPE"` before drafting).
- *(Done: The Shadow of the Master's House, Scoil Mhuire (invented), Cloncarrow (invented), County Laois, Mon 3 to Fri 14 Dec 2046; text only.)* Partial shading: 16 x 330 W 60-cell second-life panels, landscape, 2 x 8 on a 35 deg south roof, one string (about 560 V) on one input of a two-input inverter (160 V minimum); the old principal's house 7.2 m high stands 6 m forward at the west end; the assistant took the horizon from the middle of the roof (shade there only from about 15:30). Model on 14 Dec at 53.03 N: shadow on the first column from 12:55, full by 14:00; at 13:00 12 of 960 cells (1.25%) cost 11% at the high peak (6 of 48 bypass groups; landscape panels lose all three groups to a side shadow), the low peak 433 W at about 660 V; at 14:00 14.8% shaded, area guess 12.5% less, high peak 24% less, low peak 82% less (367 W, 630 V, 0.58 A); the dip between peaks passes below the 560 V restart point at about 13:50, so a shower after that strands the tracker. Dishwasher window 13:15 to 14:45: unshaded 2.99 kWh, area guess 2.65, high peak 2.39, shower at 14:00 1.68; end two unplugged 2.41/1.68 (no better); split 10 + 6 about 2.06 (the six fall under 160 V); shade scan on plus end two moved to a ballasted frame on the kitchen's flat roof 2.78. About 20 kWh (about 8 euro) lost over 19 bright afternoons; Bernie's moved afternoons the real cost.
- *(Done: The Rod by the Vestry Door, St Wilfrid's, Kelderby (invented), North Yorkshire, Mar to Jul 2047; text only.)* From the 2026-11-15 earthing proposal. Dwight single rod R = ρ/(2πL)(ln(4L/a) − 1): new 16 mm rod refused at 1.2 m in dry rubble fill (ρ 800 Ω·m, estimate) 573 Ω; second rod 1.5 m away 327 Ω; about 100 rods 2.4 m apart to reach 10 Ω. Old 12.7 mm rods 2.4 m deep in wet clay by a field drain (ρ 30) 12.6 Ω each, two 5 m apart 6.7, three 4.7; clay doubled for August 13.5 and 9.4. Rise at 30 kA: 17 MV, 9.8 MV, 141 kV; side flash to a mains-earthed chime motor and organ blower; Al 25×3 (75 mm² against 70) to buried Cu via a bimetallic plate at a test joint above ground; strike once in about 50 to 160 years (9πH², Ng 0.3 to 0.5). No turn in the north churchyard because of unmarked graves; the drain line was never used for burial.
- *(Done: The Field That Held the Water Back, Kilnavarra, County Westmeath, 2049.)* *Proposal* (2026-11-15): a culvert sized from an old rainfall table. A village's assistant replaces a collapsed stone culvert under the lane to a church and its school with a concrete pipe sized by the rational method (peak flow = runoff coefficient × rainfall intensity × catchment area) and Manning's formula, using a design storm intensity for a 1-in-30-year return period from a table published decades before, and a runoff coefficient for the fields the catchment was when the table was made, not the new estate's roofs and drives at its top. Work out before drafting, for one named catchment (area in hectares, time of concentration from its length and slope, the coefficient for pasture against the estate's share of hard surface), the peak flow then and now, the pipe's full-bore capacity at its gradient, and the real return period of the storm that overtops it once the estate is counted; state any rainfall figure as an estimate unless it is checked against a real depth-duration-frequency source, and give an uplift for a warming climate only as a range. Show a failed first fix (a bigger pipe under the lane, which floods the next culvert downstream instead) before the right one (attenuation at the estate: a pond or tank that holds the peak back, sized by the same sums). Keep it hopeful: nobody drowns; a school run is diverted once. Check overlap first (`node overlap.mjs "rational method" "return period" "Manning" "attenuation"`).
- *(Done: The Wall Down the Middle, Kilcarrig, County Clare, 2051; 8 m glulam span, 10.9 Hz empty and 8.3 Hz with 64 dancers, slower band onto the second harmonic of the heels, doubled joists to about 10 Hz rejected as the polkas' frequency, steel beam on posts at mid-span to about 20 Hz; the proposal's 'two steps a second near the natural frequency' was changed because a floor that passes the static check cannot ring that low, so the beat reaches it through the second harmonic of the heels; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Proposal* (2026-11-15): a parish hall floor that bounces at a céilí. A parish's assistant is asked whether the timber floor of a 1950s hall, refurbished as a community space, is strong enough for a set-dancing night, and checks it the way a static load table does (kilonewtons per square metre against the joists' bending and deflection) and passes it. On the night the floor is strong but lively: a reel danced at about two steps a second, by a full set of eight in step, sits near the floor's own natural frequency, and the floor visibly bounces, the tea urn walks along the trestle and an older dancer feels sick and sits out. Nobody is hurt and nothing breaks. Work out before drafting, for one invented floor (joist size, spacing, span, timber grade, the boards), the static capacity against the imposed load for a dance hall, the mid-span deflection under that load, the floor's fundamental frequency from the simply supported beam formula with the dancers' mass included or not, and the dancing pace in steps a second that lands on it (and on half of it); give damping and any acceleration limit for comfort only as ranges from the literature, stated as estimates. Show a failed first fix (asking the band to play slower, which moves the beat onto the second harmonic, or one extra joist that barely shifts the frequency) before the right one (stiffening the span with a steel beam or a line of props under the middle in the crawl space, which halves the span and raises the frequency by about four times), sized by the same sums. Check overlap first (`node overlap.mjs "natural frequency" "joist" "deflection" "céilí"`).
- *(Done: Twenty-Four a Minute, Peñaescrita (invented), Sierra de Gúdar, Teruel, Dec 2047 to Dec 2048; text only.)* Stair 2.4 m wide, two flights of 16 risers and a 2.4 m landing (27.4 m²); 420 people; guidance taken at 70 per metre per minute (168 a minute, 2.5 minutes; 'under four minutes'); on the night the outlet was the priest's handshake at the foot, groups of 2.8 at 7 s, 24 a minute, so 17.5 minutes to clear and a queue at 2.8 per m² (four abreast, one on every other step) holding about 76 on the stair; side door to the landing at the Easter Vigil (290 people, 12.1 minutes either way, 3.5 per m² at the merge, one man down on one knee) the failed fix; priest back to the porch (belén moved inside to the chapel of San Antón), chocolate to the fountain 22 m away, half the congregation greeting at 24 a minute in 8.5 minutes, the stair at a peak of 94 a minute (about 0.7 per m²), about 9 minutes in all. *Proposal* (2026-11-15): the stair after Midnight Mass. A hill-town church (invented; set it outside Ireland, since the last two stories were Irish) is reached from the square by one stone stair, and a parish's events assistant estimates how long the church takes to empty after the Christmas Midnight Mass from the stair's width and a flow rate per metre of width taken from crowd-flow guidance, and passes it. On the night the stair empties far slower than the sum said and people stand packed on the steps in the cold for twenty minutes, because the flow figure is for a stair that people keep moving on, and at the foot of this one the priest is shaking hands, the hot-chocolate stall has a queue across the landing, and families stop to find each other: the stair's capacity is set by its outlet, not its width. Nobody is hurt. Work out before drafting, for one invented stair (width, number of steps, landings) and congregation (about four hundred), the emptying time from a flow rate per metre of width (give the guidance figures for stairs only as ranges, as estimates, and check any you name against their source), the density on the steps when the outlet flow falls below the inlet flow, and how long the queue takes to clear at the real outlet rate. Show a failed first fix (opening the side door, which feeds the same stair from a second direction and makes the landing worse) before the right one (moving the handshakes and the stall up into the church porch and the square's far side, so that the foot of the stair stays clear), sized by the same sums. Keep crowd danger in proportion: this is discomfort and a near miss, not a crush, and the story must not make crowds frightening. Check overlap first (`node overlap.mjs "flow rate" "stair" "egress" "Midnight Mass"`).
- *(Done: Eleven Degrees to the Glass, El Ojito (invented), Conejos County, San Luis Valley, Colorado, 37.1° N, 2,390 m, 14 January to 2 February 2049.)* Twelve 430 W panels on each 20° slope of an east–west tin roof; clear January day 25.3 kWh south, 7.1 north. Night: air −18 °C, RH 80%, frost point −18.5 °C, Swinbank sky −48 °C (story: about −45, range −40 to −50), glass 7 to 9 °C below the air, net loss 45 to 60 W/m², about 120 g of frost a panel. Sun 14 January: 16.0° at 09:00, 23.7° at 10:00, 29.0° at 11:00, 31.5° at solar noon 12:12; on the north slope behind the plane until about 08:40, 11.4° to the glass at noon. Clearing by energy balance: south 09:16 (08:59 to 09:41), north 12:37 (11:44 to never), 65° south wall 08:30. Frost costs about 6 kWh on a frost morning (4.6 to 9.3), about 120 kWh in a January (80 to 220). Failed fix (deviation from the proposal, which suggested tilting the north panels flatter: the sky fraction only goes from 0.97 to 1.0, a few tenths of a degree, so it is not a real failure mode): the inverter's snow-melt mode, 400 W a panel from 07:00, clears at 09:06, draws 10.1 kWh to win 3.0, against a naive 1.7 kWh estimate that left out losses to the sky and air while warm. Right fix: six panels on a cedar frame (an old corral) at 65° on the hall's south wall, +2.4 kWh a panel a day in January, −1.4 in June; the solana bench moves to the west wall. Candlemas 2049 (a Tuesday): wall clear 08:29, 6.1 kWh by 11:30.
- *(Done: A Ninth of the River, Jasikovac on the Sava, Croatia, 2052.)* *Proposal* (2026-11-16): the ferry that needs the river. A village on a wide, slow lowland river (invented; not Spain, Ireland or Colorado, which the last four stories used) keeps a cable reaction ferry, a pontoon held to an overhead wire that crosses with no engine by being angled to the current, and the parish's events assistant plans the feast-day pilgrimage to the shrine on the far bank from the ferry's usual crossing time. In the dry late summer of the feast the current is a third of its spring speed, and since the sideways push on the hull grows with the square of the current, the crossing takes far longer than a third more. Work out before drafting, for one invented ferry (pontoon size, load, wire length, river width, current at spring and late-summer flow), the push on the hull as the angle and the current change, the crossing time at each flow, the pilgrims carried an hour, and what a ferryman's oar or a small outboard adds. Show a failed first fix (a steeper angle to the current, which the sums show helps less than it seems) before the right one. Check overlap first (`node overlap.mjs "reaction ferry" "overhead wire" "pontoon"`), and read *Aim Off* so the angle-to-a-current sum is not repeated.
- *(Done: Six Falls and a Sixth, Kariamu (invented) near Nyahururu, Laikipia, Kenya, 7 to 19 September 2065; text only.)* Granite altar table 2.10 × 0.95 × 0.17 m, 899 kg, 934.7 kg rigged; lift 0.925 m to clear an 0.85 m stipes, 1.10 m across on a 2 t beam trolley. Two plain-bushed triple blocks, six falls plus a floor snatch block, 9% loss a sheave: real advantage 4.37 (3.98 with the snatch), pull 235 kgf against the planned 156 (six volunteers at 26 each). Hanging scale in the hauling line: 207 kgf peak, no lift, so advantage below 4.52 and at least 6% lost a sheave. Failed fix: more sheaves (eight falls 192 kgf, twelve 150, unlimited about 102; 7.4 m of rope to haul). Lowering through the same tackle about 100 kgf (friction a thief going up, a friend coming down). Right fix: a garage's 2 t chain hoist (invented plate: 36 kgf at rated load, 68 m of hand chain a metre), about 17 kgf, 63 m of chain, about three minutes of pulling in a 51 minute lift; beam load 965 kg with the hoist against 1,170 with the tackle. Yellow line on the floor, nobody under the load. *Proposal* (2026-11-16): the tackle that gives less than it counts. A mountain parish outside Europe (say the Kenyan highlands or the Chilean south; recent stories have been European and Philippine) must lift a stone altar slab of about 900 kg onto a raised sanctuary, or a restored organ chest into a loft, with no crane able to reach, and the parish's assistant plans it with a borrowed block and tackle: six falls of rope, so a sixth of the weight on the hauling line, so four volunteers. Each sheave loses some of the pull to friction (published figures run about 5 to 10% a sheave for plain bearings), and the losses compound, so the real advantage is well under six and the volunteers find the load will not leave the floor. Work out before drafting, for one invented lift (load, rope, sheave count and losses, safe working load of the rope and the anchor, what a person can pull steadily), the ideal and real advantage, the hauling force, the rope speed and lift time. Show a failed first fix (more sheaves, which the sums show add almost nothing once the losses pile up and multiply the rope to be hauled) before the right one (a geared hand winch, a chain hoist, or a capstan, and a safe anchor). Check overlap first (`node overlap.mjs "block and tackle" "sheave" "winch"`), and make the safety honest: nobody stands under the load, and the story says it is fiction, not rigging advice.
- *(Done: Ten Taps at Noon, Ollamo (invented), Hadiya Zone, Ethiopia, Sat 15 to Sat 29 Sep 2063; spring tank 24 m above the taps, 760 m of 32 mm PE (27.2 mm ID), Hazen-Williams C 145, 0.478 L/s with the whole drop vs a 0.44 L/s lunch average; ten taps 1.0 m left, 0.047 L/s each, 0.47 together; +0.8 m at the spring gives 2%; 3 m³ day tank 7.0 m up on 30 m of 50 mm, 0.12 to 0.13 L/s a tap, refilled at 0.38 L/s, lowest 2.11 m³ in a minute-by-minute feast model; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Proposal* (2026-11-16): the pipe that sings at the wrong hour. A parish in the Andes or the Ethiopian highlands (invented; not Kenya, Ireland, Spain or Croatia, which recent stories used) runs its new hall's water by gravity from a spring tank on the hill, and the parish assistant sizes the pipe for the peak demand of a feast-day lunch from the drop and the length alone. On the day, the taps at the far end of the hall run to a trickle at noon, because friction in a long small pipe grows with roughly the square of the flow (Darcy–Weisbach or Hazen–Williams), so the head left at the far taps falls fastest just when everyone opens them. Work out before drafting, for one invented system (spring tank height, pipe length and bore, number of taps and their flow), the head loss at low and peak flow, the flow at the farthest tap, and how long a lunch for a few hundred people takes to wash up. Show a failed first fix (a larger tank at the spring, which adds almost no head) before the right one (a break tank or a day tank near the hall, filled slowly all morning, or a second, larger pipe), sized by the same sums. Check overlap first (`node overlap.mjs "head loss" "Hazen" "gravity main" "spring tank"`).
- *(Done: The Gallery That Is Full for the First Time, São Miguel do Brejo Alto, Minas Gerais, 2026-11-16; an overhang of 1.8 m beyond a beam on a 4.0 m back span rather than a pure cantilever, notched joists over the beam, rating 2.5 kN/m², "room for 146", reopening crowd 1.21 of the design figure, laser rises 8 mm with the root moment unchanged, rope at 1 m and 143 people, props deferred to the engineer.)* *Proposal* (2026-11-16): the gallery that is full for the first time. A town church in Brazil, the Philippines or Poland (invented; not Ethiopia, Kenya, Peru or Sicily, which recent stories used) reopens its timber choir gallery for a jubilee, and the parish assistant checks it by dividing the gallery's rated floor load (kN/m² over its area) by the weight of a person, so it reports "room for 120". A retired carpenter says the gallery was never the floor: it is the two cantilevered joists at the front, where people crowd to see the procession, and a load concentrated at the tip of a cantilever bends it (and stresses its root) far more than the same load spread evenly. Work out before drafting, for one invented gallery (span, joist section and timber grade, number of joists, the assumed person and crowd densities from a building code's published imposed loads), the bending moment and tip deflection for an even load and for a crowd packed into the front metre, and how many people the front can take. Show a failed first fix (more people at the back to "balance" it, which a cantilever does not do) before the right one (a rope at the front metre and a steward, or a temporary prop sized by the same sums). Nothing in it is structural advice, and the note says so. Check overlap first (`node overlap.mjs "cantilever" "imposed load" "choir gallery"`).
- *(Done: Six Times for a Second, Saint Raphael's Health Centre, Nyobiisi (invented), Upper East Region, Ghana, Sun 15 Jan to Fri 17 Mar 2051; text only.)* Nameplate sum 5.5 + 3.0 + 3.5 = 12 kW plus a third gives a 20 kVA (16 kW) set; the 5.5 kW submersible draws 11.5 A running and 69 A (6x) locked-rotor, 47.9 kVA, so with X'd 0.25 the dip is 37% (torque 0.39) and the engine needs 16.8 kW alone, 24.0 kW with the 3 kW compressor (28.1 kVA start, 26% dip, the blinking lights) and other loads on, and trips on underfrequency; true running 13.8 kW / 16.6 kVA (a sixth to spare, not a third); pump-first works once and fails when the float calls at 01:40; a DOL-capable set would be 48 kVA (a fifty at a third load, wet stacking), a soft starter at 3x gives 24 kVA and 23%, a 7.5 kW drive 7.1 kVA and 8% (set 2.8x the drive), with an output reactor for 80 m of cable and a 30 Hz-in-1 s ramp for the thrust bearing; cold room 8 h 20 min off to 10.2 C, outreach put back a week, oxytocin written off, the delivery-bed fund pays; Daniel Abugri (b. 1983, motor rewinder 2005-2043), Sister Mercy Atinga, Gifty Azure, Simon Akolgo, Fr Augustine Ayamga, Issah Mahama, assistant Daybook. Original proposal: the pump that will not start on the generator. A rural parish or mission clinic in Chile, Indonesia or Ghana (invented; not Brazil, Ireland or the Philippines, which recent stories used) buys a backup generator for its borehole pump and cold room, and the assistant sizes it by adding up the running watts on each nameplate, with a margin, so the generator should carry everything with a third to spare. At the first power cut the cold room runs and the pump will not start: the generator's voltage sags and its breaker trips, because an induction motor starting across the line draws several times its running current for a second or two. Work out before drafting, for one invented site (pump and compressor motor ratings, a published locked-rotor current multiple, the generator's rating and its allowable voltage dip), the starting kVA against what the generator can supply, and show a failed first fix (starting the pump first, before the cold room, which helps less than people expect, since the motor's own start is the peak) before the right one (a soft starter or a variable-speed drive on the pump, sized by the same sums, or a larger set at a cost the story counts). Nothing in it is electrical advice, and the note says so. Overlap checked 2026-11-16: 'inrush', 'starting current', 'locked rotor' and 'soft starter' unused; 'generator' is common, so check the stories that pair it with a motor before drafting.
- *(Done: What the Night Air Lends, Colonia Santa Rosa (invented), near Nogoyá, Entre Ríos, Argentina, Wed 9 May to Mon 18 Jun 2063; text only.)* 160 t of maize at 17.5% in a 9 m bin, 3.5 m deep, 5.6 t of water to 14.5%; modified Henderson EMC (dent constants) in a 35-layer equilibrium deep-bed model: one fan at 1 m³/min/t continuously removes about 190 kg/day in ordinary May weather (EMC 11.1% at 21 °C/49%, 15.0% at the daily mean) and the front climbs about 10 cm/day; a two-week fog spell puts back about 400 kg and the bottom goes 13.5% to 18%; the proposal's fix (fans only when air EMC is below the grain) is the failed first attempt here: 47 fan-hours in 6 days, front stalls, top safe 8 July (continuous: 6 July) against a seven-week storage clock ending 25 June, and the bin overdries to 12.6% (3.4 t less to sell); fog at 13.7 °C saturated leaves a dry bottom metre at 15.6 °C and 61% (heat of adsorption), leaving 31 kg/h there; the fix is twice the air continuously (603 Pa vs 232 Pa, five times the air power, a borrowed 7.5 kW fan) plus coring 4 t of fines: top 15% on 17 June, 14.5% on 18 June, bin 14.3%; Chung-Pfost constants give a drier bin and a single fan in time, so the assistant calibrates on the 15 May sample (13.5%, Henderson 13.5, Chung-Pfost 12.1). Ramón Villalba (b. 1989, elevator hand 1999-2040), Lucía and Joaquín Benítez, Fr Matías Ferreyra, assistant Tolva. Original proposal: the fans that wet the maize. A parish farm co-operative in the Brazilian south, Argentina or Zambia (invented; not Poland, Ethiopia, Kenya or Croatia, which recent stories used) stores its maize harvest in a bin with a perforated floor and fans, to feed a soup kitchen through the year, and the co-op's assistant runs the fans round the clock because moving air dries grain. In the humid nights of a wet autumn the night air's equilibrium moisture content for maize is above the grain's, so the fans put water back in, and the top of the bin starts to warm and smell. Work out before drafting, from a published equilibrium moisture content equation for maize (for example the modified Henderson or Chung-Pfost forms with published constants) and one invented bin (depth, tonnes, airflow in cubic metres a minute a tonne, starting and safe moisture), the grain's equilibrium moisture at day and night air conditions, the water each fan-hour adds or removes, the drying front's speed, and how long until the top layer is safe. Show a failed first fix (more fans, or a bigger fan, which dries the bottom faster and wets nothing less at night) before the right one (a humidistat that runs the fans only when the air's equilibrium moisture is below the target, or a little heat), sized by the same sums. Check overlap first (`node overlap.mjs "equilibrium moisture" "aeration" "grain bin"`), and read *The Hay That Warms Itself* so the self-heating is not repeated as the centre.
- *(Done: The Digester That Went Sour, near Mityana, Uganda, 2052.)* *Proposal* (2026-11-16): the digester that went sour. A mission school or parish farm in Uganda or Tanzania (invented), India or Peru (invented; not Argentina or Ghana, which the last two stories used) feeds a household biogas digester with the kitchen's waste to cook the boarders' porridge, and in the hungry season the kitchen staff are told to feed it more, because more feed should mean more gas. The gas falls and then stops; the slurry smells of vinegar. Work out before drafting, for one invented fixed-dome digester (volume, daily feed in kilograms of volatile solids, retention time), the organic loading rate against a published safe range for small mesophilic digesters, how the acids build when the methanogens are outpaced, the pH and the gas yield per kilogram of volatile solids at the right load, and the days to recover. Show a failed first fix (feeding it lime alone, which lifts the pH for a day without fixing the load) before the right one (stop feeding, add buffered water or manure from a healthy digester, then raise the feed slowly), sized by the same sums, and count the charcoal bought in the meantime. Nothing in it is engineering or safety advice, and the note says so (biogas is flammable and the gas in a digester's dome can asphyxiate). Overlap checked 2026-11-16: 'biogas' and 'anaerobic digester' unused.
- *(Done: A Metre Apart, Lisnageeha (invented), Kilcarra, east Galway, 3 Mar to Sun 3 Jun 2046; text only.)* *A cillín counted by radar* (proposed 2026-11-17): east Galway, 2046. A parish wants to fence and bless a children's burial ground (a cillín, a recorded monument) on a farm before the cattle go out. A licensed ground-penetrating radar survey's first pass runs lines a metre apart east-west, parallel to the graves, and finds nine; nine is a floor, not a count. A grave cut about 35 cm wide, seen about 10 cm beyond each edge, is crossed by a line with probability about 0.55, so nine found means about sixteen (12 to 24, nine times in ten, flat prior). Depth from two-way time at 0.08 m/ns (10 ns is 40 cm). Offsetting a convex fence outward by d adds 2*pi*d to its length whatever its shape. The second pass (north-south, 25 cm) finds 15 inside and 2 just outside the bank where the posts would have gone. No number on the stone.
- *(Done: The Old Man Under the Nave, Strieborná Lehota (invented), Štiavnica Mountains, Slovakia, Sun 23 Sep to Sun 25 Nov 2063; text only.)* *Muons under the nave* (proposed 2026-11-17): a mining village in the Štiavnica Mountains, Slovakia (invented), 2063. The nave floor of the parish church cracks after a wet September and the engineer closes it; an 18th-century mine map shows an old working (*Alter Mann*) about 18 m under the floor, a mining fathom high, and a collapse can climb about ten times a working's height. A loaned muon tracker in the drainage adit 40 m below counts the cosmic-ray muons that get through the rock (about one per thumbnail a minute at the surface, about one every half hour down there); a void lets a few more through. The first map shows a false void that is the cemetery terrace's rubble fill; the real gallery is six metres from where the map drew it. The engineer's question is not what the detector found but what it could have missed. Cost: the gallery, with a cross cut in its wall, is grouted shut.
- *(Done: The Joint That Is Still Pink, Csík basin, Romania, 2026-11-17.)* *Lime that is still setting* (proposed 2026-11-17): a mason repoints a medieval church tower with lime mortar on a deadline (a wedding, a bell's return), and the parish council wants it painted or loaded at the end of the week because the mortar "feels hard". Lime hardens by taking carbon dioxide from the air, from the face inwards, and the carbonated depth grows roughly as the square root of time, so the mortar deep in a joint can still be soft for months, and a joint twice as deep takes four times as long. Work the rate from published carbonation measurements, the joint depths and the wet weather that slows it; the phenolphthalein spray on a broken sample (pink where it is still lime) is the scene. Not Slovakia or Galway, which the last two stories used.
- *(Done: As Cold as the Shed, Nishiokawa (invented), Kamikawa Subprefecture, Hokkaido, 10 January to 2 February 2048; text only.)* *Ice along the eaves* (proposed 2026-11-17).  Ice dam on a steel-sheet parish hall roof held by snow guards: ceiling U 0.20 + leakage 0.30 (hatch, 12 light fittings) into the roof space, snow 0.30 m at 0.1 W/m·K (R 3.0), deck R 0.2, vents blocked 0.05 vs baffled 2.0 W/m²K; at 20 °C in, −8 °C out the deck is clamped at 0 °C, roof space +1.7 °C, net melt 6.0 W/m² (0.064 mm/h), about 0.67 kg/m² a day over 90 m², 60 L/day to a 15 m eave; melting down to about −24 °C outside before, only above about −2 °C after; raking the eave moves the dam up a metre; ceiling loss about 31% lower after.
- *(Done: The Weight in the Belfry, Wessenbrock (invented), near Cloppenburg, Lower Saxony, Sat 14 Apr to Thu 1 Nov 2063; text only.)* A 3,300 kg bell (k = 0.47, L_eq 1.18 m) at 70° swings 2.402 s (0.4164 Hz); the sideways force m g k sin θ (3 cos θ − 2 cos Θ) has only odd harmonics (the proposal's 'swing rate and twice it' was wrong: twice is the vertical force), and its third, 8.69 kN at 1.2491 Hz, sits on a 1.2475 Hz brick tower (484 t at the bell chamber, 1.5% damping): 10 mm, against 0.5 mm from the first harmonic and 0.4 mm from the old 1,400 kg steel bell (third harmonic at 1.446 Hz). The council's no is a hammer striker. The proposal's physics also needed turning round: Den Hartog's settings (μ 1%, 1.235 Hz, ζ 6%, 2.4 t in the spire room counting 2.02×) are the failed first try at 3.1 mm, because the force comes at one known frequency; the fix is 4.8 t (μ 2%) tuned to the bell's 1.249 Hz at 2% damping, 1.0 mm steady, 1.5 ringing up, 1.2 down (chains 16.3 cm then 15.9 cm); the old steel bell is part of the damper mass.
- *(Done: Four Times the Wait, San Isidro de Yanayaku, Ayacucho, Peru, March 2058.)* *Four times the wait* (proposed 2026-11-17) : after a flood, a village's water comes from a river so full of fine silt that the filters clog in an hour, and the plan is to let it stand in a tank first. Stokes's law, v = (2/9) Δρ g r² / μ, says a grain half the size settles four times slower and cold water (viscosity about 1.31 against 1.00 mPa·s at 10 and 20 °C) slows it by about a third: with Δρ 1,650 kg/m³, 10 µm silt falls 1.5 m in about 4.6 hours at 20 °C and 6.1 at 10 °C, 5 µm silt in about 18.5 and 24 hours. The reasonable no is the night shift that wants to draw water at dawn after six hours; the failed first try is a tank stirred by its own inflow. Work the grain sizes from a real sieve-and-settling test, the tank depth, and how a dose of alum or a little salt (flocculation) makes small grains act like big ones, stated as an assumption until sourced. 'Stokes' and 'sedimentation' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; *The Water That Walks Up* is frost heave in silt, so keep the silt in the water; not Germany, Japan or Galway.
- *(Done: Twice as Long, a Quarter the Load, Fusha e Arrave, Zadrima, Albania, November 2051.)* *Twice as long, a quarter the load* (proposed 2026-11-17): after an earthquake a parish shores a cracked chancel arch with whatever steel it has, and the volunteers trust a scaffold tube because it held a tractor on a chain. A 48.3 mm by 4 mm steel tube (area 557 mm², I = 137,700 mm⁴, E 210 GPa) yields at about 131 kN in tension at 235 MPa, but as a pinned strut Euler's load π²EI/L² is 127 kN at 1.5 m, 71 at 2 m, 32 at 3 m and 8 at 6 m: twice as long, a quarter the load, and the arch needs props about 5 m long. The reasonable no is the diocesan engineer who will not come for a week and says touch nothing; the failed first try is one long prop that bows and is caught in time. The fix is shorter props with lacing tubes halving the free length, and a real safety factor stated as an assumption. Work the arch load, the eccentricity (real props fail well below Euler) and the bracing before drafting; say plainly that shoring is a job for engineers. 'Buckling' is unused in `overlap.mjs`; Euler appears once (*Three Turns and a Child*, check its sense); not Peru, Germany, Japan or Galway.
- *(Done: The Line on the Ceiling, Seaburn Dene, Sunderland, 2049; text only. Beam as a one-dimensional fin: steel at the wall 7 to 9 C at 0 C outside, mould zone 14 to 26 cm, wet zone 5 to 12 cm; fixes: wrap the back-span 60 cm, humidity-controlled fan to about 50% RH, thermal break deferred.)* *The line on the ceiling* (proposed 2026-11-17): a parish insulates its presbytery's walls inside and a straight black line of mould appears on the ceiling of the new curate's bedroom by February, exactly under a steel beam that runs out through the insulation to carry the porch. Steel conducts heat about 1,400 times better than mineral wool (about 50 against 0.035 W/m·K), so the beam is a cold bridge. With the room at 20 °C and 60% relative humidity the dew point is about 12.0 °C, but mould needs only about 80% humidity at the surface, which happens below about 15.4 °C: a surface temperature factor (inside surface above outside, over inside above outside) of 0.77 at 0 °C outside, near the 0.75 that UK guidance uses. The reasonable no is the builder who says the insulation is to spec; the failed first try is bleach and anti-mould paint. Work the beam's heat path (a simple two-dimensional estimate or a published psi-value), the surface temperature and the fix (wrapping the beam's inner end, more ventilation, a cut thermal break) before drafting, stated as assumptions where not sourced. 'Thermal bridge' and 'condensation' are unused in `overlap.mjs` ('mould' appears once, in *Sixty-Two in a Hundred*); not Peru, Albania, Germany, Japan or Galway.
- *(Done: The Pump That Would Not Rest, Rīga, 2026-11-18.)* *The pump that would not rest* (proposed 2026-11-18): a crypt chapel below the water table keeps dry with a sump pump, and after a volunteer replaces the old float switch with a cheap electronic level sensor the pump burns out a motor every winter. The new sensor switches on and off at nearly the same level: with a 0.5 m² pit, 0.5 L/s of seepage and a 2 L/s pump, a 2 cm band is 10 litres, about 7 s running and 20 s resting, about 130 starts an hour, where a small submersible motor is commonly rated for a few tens; a 20 cm band gives about 13. The reasonable no is the sacristan who says the old float was ugly and stuck; the failed first try is a bigger pump, which shortens the cycles. Work the cycle times, a motor's rated starts per hour (from a real datasheet, or stated as an assumption) and the fixes (a wider band, a time delay, a bigger pit) before drafting. 'Hysteresis' is unused in `overlap.mjs`; *The Short Night on the Backup Battery* has a sump pump on battery (Peukert), a different angle.
- *(Done: The Tide Mark That Climbed, Bouça da Veiga, Minho, Portugal, 2026-11-18.)* *The tide mark that climbed* (proposed 2026-11-18): a parish renders the damp lower walls of a lime-built chapel with cement to "seal the damp out", and within two years the tide mark of salts and flaking paint inside has climbed from knee to shoulder height. In the sharp-front model of capillary rise (Hall and Hoff, *Water Transport in Brick, Stone and Concrete*) the steady height a wall wets to scales roughly as sorptivity times the square root of wall thickness over evaporation rate, so cutting the wall's drying surface by a cement skin on one face raises the front, and quartering evaporation about doubles it; check that form and its constants against the source before drafting, and state the wall's sorptivity and evaporation as assumptions. The reasonable no is a council member who paid for the render and points out it is waterproof; the failed first try is a chemical damp-proof injection that does nothing while the render stays. The fix is taking the render off and a lime render or none, with ground levels and a drain outside, and the cost is the plaster that has to come off with it. 'Rising damp' and 'cement render' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; not Peru, Albania, Germany, Japan or Galway.
- *(Done: The Wave That Stands Still, Camp St. Isidore on the invented Cedar Fork, Indiana, July 2058.)* *The wave that stands still* (proposed 2026-11-18): a parish youth camp beside a river with an old low mill weir, where the smooth hump of water below the sill looks like the gentlest place to play, and is a hydraulic jump with a recirculating roller that holds a swimmer against the face. Work it before drafting: with an assumed 2 m² of flow per metre of weir and 0.25 m of fast water at the toe (8 m/s, Froude number about 5.1), the Bélanger relation y₂/y₁ = ½(√(1 + 8Fr²) − 1) gives about 6.7, a 1.7 m deep pool, and the jump loses about 1.75 m of head, roughly 34 kW for every metre of weir width; state the flow and toe depth as assumptions and check the relation and the low-head-dam drowning record in a hydraulics text and a water-safety body's guidance (not invented statistics). The reasonable no is the camp leader who swam there as a boy in a dry summer; the failed first try is a buoy line upstream of the weir, which keeps boats off the sill but not children wading in below it. The fix is that nobody goes in downstream within the roller's reach, a throw line on the bank and the reason told plainly, and the cost is the afternoon swim the camp was built around. 'Hydraulic jump', 'weir' and 'low-head' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; not Portugal, Peru, Albania, Germany, Japan or Galway.
- *(Done: The Skin on the Sand, St Monica's Mission, Kaombe, Lake Malawi, 2026-11-18.)* *The skin on the sand* (proposed 2026-11-18): a mission village's slow sand filter, two concrete beds feeding the school and about 1,400 people, is scraped every Saturday by a new and conscientious caretaker who thinks the brown slime on top is the dirt the filter exists to remove; it is the schmutzdecke, the living layer that does most of the work, and after each scraping the filter needs days to weeks to ripen again. Work it before drafting: at a filtration rate of 0.15 m/h (inside the 0.1 to 0.3 m/h range design guides give; check WHO or another water body's manual), 20 m² of bed gives 3 m³ an hour, about 72 m³ a day, 50 litres each for 1,400 people; scraping removes the top centimetre or two, and a common rule is to clean only when head loss reaches about a metre, typically every few weeks to months. State the ripening time and the removal before and after as assumptions from a named source, never invented percentages. The reasonable no is the caretaker's predecessor's widow, who says her husband scraped it too; the failed first try is chlorinating harder, which hides the problem in the tap and not in the bed. The fix is scraping by head loss, one bed at a time, running a newly scraped bed to waste until it ripens, and the cost is the Saturday ritual the caretaker was proud of and a fortnight of half supply. 'Slow sand', 'schmutzdecke' and 'sand filter' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Wet-Morning Trip, Bethesda, Gwynedd, 2026-11-18.)* *The wet-morning trip* (proposed 2026-11-18): the story uses a 30 mA RCD's 15 to 30 mA band (BS EN 61008-1), BS 7671:2018's 30% standing-leakage advice (9 mA) and 411.3.3, IEC 60335-1's 0.75 mA/kW (2.25 mA for 3 kW); building at rest 3.1 mA, element 16.4 mA wet (14 kΩ) and 3.9 dry (59 kΩ), convector 2.8 and 0.9; wet 22.3 mA against devices ramp-tested at 21 and 19 mA, dry 7.9; after RCBOs 3.1, 1.6 and 1.5 mA. Original proposal: a parish's converted stone outbuilding (a food bank store, say, or a sacristy and meeting room in an old coach house) loses its power at about seven on every wet morning, when a timer brings on an old water heater and a convector heater together; the residual-current device trips, the volunteer who opens up resets it, and on the worst mornings it will not stay in. The physics: every appliance leaks a little current to earth, damp mineral-insulated heating elements leak more when cold, and the leakages on one device add up; a 30 mA RCD must trip by its rated current and is allowed to trip from half of it, so a building's standing leakage can sit just under the line on a dry day and cross it on a wet one. Work it before drafting from a named standard or wiring guide (the trip band for a 30 mA device; the common advice to keep standing leakage on one RCD well below its rating; a typical leakage for a damp element versus a dry one, labelled as an assumption); add the leakages for the building's actual loads and show the wet-morning sum crossing 15 mA. The reasonable no is the churchwarden who wants the RCD swapped for a plain breaker (or 'a less sensitive one') because the old board never tripped; the failed first try is a brand-new RCD, which trips the same. The fix is measuring each circuit's leakage with a clamp meter, a dry heater element or a new one, and splitting the loads across separate RCDs or RCBOs; the cost is a winter's heating budget and the electrician's honest refusal to leave anyone unprotected. Tell the reader it is fiction and that electrical work is for a qualified person. 'Earth leakage' and 'residual-current' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; the RCD appears only in passing in *The Urn at the End of the Reel*, about voltage drop.
- *(Done: The Lamp in the Duct, St Clement's parish hall, St Paul, Minnesota, 2026-11-18.)* *The lamp in the duct* (proposed 2026-11-18): a parish hall used as a winter warm space and for a weekly clinic gets an upper-room ultraviolet (UV-C) unit, or one in the ventilation duct, to cut airborne infection, and a year later the cleaner notices the tubes are grey with dust. The physics: the germicidal dose a microbe receives is irradiance times exposure time, so it depends on how fast the air moves past the lamp and how much of the room's air goes through the beam in an hour (equivalent air changes); lamp output falls with age, with cold, and with dust on the tube. Work it before drafting from a named source (a published UV-C design guide or a peer-reviewed upper-room study for the dose a given microbe needs, labelled as an assumption where it varies; the manufacturer's output curve for age and temperature as an assumption): show the dose at installation, the dose after a year of dust and lamp ageing, and the dose with the fan turned up, which the reasonable no (a committee member who wants the fan on full 'to blow the germs out') expects to help and which can lower the dose per pass while raising the air through the beam. The failed first try is brighter tubes; the fix is a cleaning and replacement schedule written into the hall's rota, and a meter reading, not a sticker. The cost is the hall's insurance asking whether anyone is checking; the honesty rule is never to claim a figure for infections prevented that nobody measured. Upper-room UV-C is a real eye and skin hazard: the story says so, keeps the beam above head height, and says it is fiction, not a design. 'UV-C' and 'ultraviolet' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Band Above the Steps, Dumfries, Scotland, 2026-11-18.)* *The band above the steps* (proposed 2026-11-18): a sandstone church whose west steps are salted every winter so nobody falls on the way to early Mass finds the stone crumbling in a band between about knee and waist height on the wall beside them, sugary grains on the sill every spring, while the stone above and below is sound. The mechanism is salt weathering: meltwater carries sodium chloride into the porous stone, it wicks up, and it crystallises in the pores where the wall dries, so the damage sits at the drying front, not where the salt lands. Work it before drafting from a named conservation source (for example Historic England's *Practical Building Conservation* volume on stone, or a peer-reviewed paper on salt crystallisation in sandstone, for the mechanism and the height of capillary rise; the crystallisation pressure from the Correns relation as a labelled estimate, not a quoted figure): how much salt a season's gritting puts on the steps, a plausible share of it reaching the wall, and why the band is where it is. The reasonable no is the sacristan who says the salt is why no one has broken a hip in nine winters, which is true; the failed first try is a clear water-repellent sealant on the band, which moves the drying front behind the face and takes the surface off in a sheet the next spring (say so as a known conservation risk, not a certainty). The fix is grit or a different de-icer on the top steps, a gravel strip and drain between the steps and the wall, and poultices to draw the salt out over two winters, with a measured salinity before and after; the cost is a rougher, uglier step and a slower cure. 'salt weathering', 'sandstone', 'de-ic' and 'crystallis' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; *The Sack by the Lychgate* is about how much salt ice needs, a different angle.
- *(Done: The Tube That Faces the Wind, Wattle Flat (invented), New South Wales, 2046.)* *The tube that faces the wind*  (proposed 2026-11-18): a volunteer gliding club in a dry upland summer has its two-seat trainer's airspeed indicator read zero on the winch launch, and the instructor flies the circuit on attitude and the sound of the air, as gliding instructors are taught to. On the ground a mud-dauber wasp's nest is found plugging the pitot tube. Work it before drafting: the indicator measures dynamic pressure, q = ½ρv², about 200 Pa at 35 knots, 320 at 45 and 490 at 55 at sea-level density (1.225 kg/m³), so a stall margin is a few hundred pascals; indicated speed is not true speed (at 3,000 m density about 0.909 kg/m³, true airspeed is about 16% above indicated), which is why the stall is flown by the indicated figure. Check from a named source (for example the FAA Glider Flying Handbook or the Pilot's Handbook of Aeronautical Knowledge) how a blocked pitot with an open static port reads in a climb and a descent, and do not borrow a real accident's facts without saying so. The reasonable no is the club treasurer who says the glider has flown 4,000 launches without a cover and the club cannot afford a grounding in the best soaring week; the failed first try is tape over the tube overnight, which leaves glue in the hole and is forgotten once. The fix is a cover with a long red streamer, a line in the daily inspection, and a leak and pressure check with a tube and a syringe, with the number written down; the cost is a lost day's flying and a slower morning every day after. 'pitot' and 'gliding club' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Filter That Runs Slower, Las Pozas, Nicaragua, 2026-11-19.)* *The filter that runs slower*  (proposed 2026-11-18): after a river flood, a parish relief team hands out clay pot filters (a fired clay-and-sawdust pot painted with silver, sitting in a plastic bucket with a tap) to households drinking from a muddy well, and one grandmother's filter slows from a full pot in a morning to a trickle in three weeks. Work it before drafting, with a source in hand (for example the Ceramics Manufacturing Working Group's best-practice guidelines for ceramic pot filters, or published field studies): the usual new flow rate and the rate at which a filter should be scrubbed or replaced, and the instruction to clean it with a soft brush and the household's own filtered water, never soap or a hard brush. Arithmetic to check: a household of five needing about 4 litres each a day for drinking and cooking needs 20 litres; at a new filter's rate of about 2 litres an hour that is ten hours of filtering, at 0.8 litres an hour twenty-five, more than a day has, so a slowed filter is not a nuisance but a household drinking from the well again. The reasonable no is the grandmother, who will not scrub the pot her late husband carried home; the failed first try is a grandson scrubbing it hard with a wire brush and sand, which restores the flow and thins the wall and the silver that does part of the work; the fix is a jug-and-timer flow test written on the bucket every Sunday, settling the turbid water first in a second bucket, a soft brush, and a second filter for big households. Name the speculative step (none needed if set near today) and do not claim a removal percentage without the source's own figure. 'ceramic' and 'pot filter' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; *The Pot That Needs Dry Air* (pot-in-pot cooler) is a different pot, not this subject.
- *(Done: The Square That Stays Warm, Valdelobos de la Sierra (invented), Cáceres, Spain, 2026-11-19.)* *The square that stays warm* (proposed 2026-11-19): in a heatwave, a small care home on the stone-paved main square of a southern European hill town logs its bedroom temperatures at night, and the rooms that face the square stay warm long after the rooms facing the garden have cooled. Work it before drafting, with a source in hand (for example T. R. Oke's work on the urban heat island, or a national weather service's page on it and on 'tropical nights', nights whose minimum stays at or above 20 °C): how much warmer a small town's paved centre typically stays than the fields around it on a calm, clear night (a degree or a few, not the large-city figure), why the gap peaks at night (stone and tarmac give back the day's heat, and the narrow street sees less open sky to radiate to), and why it shrinks in wind or cloud. Arithmetic to check from the home's own log, worked day by day in code: hourly readings in a square-side room and a garden-side room, and the hour each night at which the outside air on each side drops below the room, which is the hour opening that side's window starts to cool the room instead of warming it. The reasonable no is the matron who will not move frail residents out of the rooms they know; the failed first try is the night staff opening every window at ten in the evening, which lets the warmer square air into the front rooms; the fix is a window rule by side and by hour written from the log, shade and wet curtains by day, and the two most heat-vulnerable residents moved to garden rooms with their own things. Name the speculative step (none needed if set near today), state no health threshold the source does not give, and say in the note that the story is not medical advice. 'heat island', 'tropical night' and 'care home' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Drum That Filled Backwards, St Joseph's Farm School, Thiruvarulpuram (invented), Thoothukudi district, Tamil Nadu, 27 Apr to 2 May 2061; text only.)* *The drum that filled backwards* (proposed 2026-11-19): a Venturi fertiliser injector on a solar drip line. Ideal Bernoulli and continuity, 40 mm pipe, 10 mm throat, 70% cone recovery assumed: at 1.5 L/s the throat runs 19.1 m/s, speed pressure 1.82 bar against 1.6 bar at the inlet (0.4 bar static for a 4 m rise plus 1.2 bar x (Q/1.5)^2), a pull of 0.22 bar (2.2 m of water); the pull starts at about 1.21 L/s and at 1.0 L/s the throat is 0.13 bar above the air, so the morning line fills the drum (180 L at 09:30, 210 at 10:00, empty 11:43 on a 0.5 to 1.5 L/s ramp, 4 mm port, Cd 0.6). A 14 mm throat (9.7 m/s, 0.47 bar) sits 1.13 bar above the air, pushes back about 7 L/min, brims a 218 L drum from 150 L at about 11:10 (28 L over by 11:14), and never pulls against that field at any flow. The injector costs about half a bar (outlet 1.06); top rows get about three quarters of their flow on the model. Fix: the 10 mm on the old bypass loop (the manager's 'jug handle'), a non-return valve, a red line at 1.3 L/s (reached about 10:12; 200 L empty about 11:45).

The second half is the monsoon's first humid week in July: the cooler cannot get its air below the wet bulb, the ward runs about 35 °C at over 60% humidity, and a second cooler would take the thermometer down two degrees but the ward's wet bulb under one; the cooler man talks himself out of the sale, and the money buys a small heat pump for the consulting room for the frailest four. Simplified steady-state model in the note (pad effectiveness 0.8, Stull's wet-bulb fit, 6.5 kW sensible load). 'Evaporative cooler' and 'swamp cooler' are unused in `overlap.mjs` (the subject comes from `subjects.txt`); *The Pot That Needs Dry Air* covers the wet-bulb limit for a pot of medicine, and this one turns on airflow through a room and the humid week.
- *(Done: The Wall That Is Warm at Nine, Pampa Phuju near Mazocruz, Puno, Peru, 2026-11-19.)* *The wall that is warm at nine*  (proposed 2026-11-19): a high-altitude village school (somewhere with clear winter skies and cold nights, for example the Ladakh or Altiplano plateau; check the setting's sun and temperatures from a named source) adds a Trombe wall, a dark masonry wall behind glass on the sunny side, so the classroom is warm without fuel. Work it before drafting: for a periodic daily heat wave through solid masonry the time lag is about thickness divided by the square root of (2 × thermal diffusivity × the daily angular frequency), so with concrete-like diffusivity near 7 × 10⁻⁷ m²/s a 30 cm wall delays the afternoon heat by about 8 hours and damps the swing to roughly a ninth (e to the minus 2.2); a 10 cm wall delays it under 3 hours. That is the plot: the wall's heat arrives in the evening, when the school is empty, unless the room is used at night (an evening literacy class, a boarding dormitory) or the wall is thinner with vents for daytime air. The reasonable no is the head teacher who wants the money for a stove; the failed first try is a wall that warms an empty room at nine and loses it all back through the glass by dawn because nobody shutters the glass at night; the fix pairs the wall with whoever uses the room after dark and an insulating night shutter. Work it before drafting: Newton's law of cooling for a pot in a box, T(t) = T_air + (T0 − T_air)·e^(−t/τ), with τ set by the pot's heat capacity over the box's heat loss, so that a pot half the size, or half full, cools roughly twice as fast through the same insulation; model a full 40-litre pot and a 10-litre pot through four hours with stated box conductances, and check the time above a hot-holding temperature against a named food-safety source (for example the WHO's *Five Keys to Safer Food*, which advises keeping cooked food very hot, above 60 C; check the wording first) and the time beans need near the boil to soften. The reasonable no is the cook who says the box was meant for the big pots and the fire is cheaper than a sick child; the failed first try is the small pots served lukewarm and sent back to the fire; the fix is a box sized to the pot, the small pots packed together in one box, and a reboil before serving written on the kitchen wall. - *(Done: The Net That Stopped Killing, Kyamugaya, Kagera, Tanzania, 2026-11-20; text only.)* *The net that stopped killing* (proposed 2026-11-20): a parish health committee in a malarious district (pick a real region where pyrethroid resistance in the local *Anopheles* has been reported in published surveillance, and check it) counts its success in bed nets handed out and slept under, and the counts are excellent, and the clinic's malaria cases climb anyway. The catechist who runs the net drive is sure the families are not using them. Work it before drafting, from a named source (for example the WHO *Manual for monitoring insecticide resistance in mosquito vectors and selecting appropriate interventions*, 2022, or the WHO *Test procedures for insecticide resistance monitoring in malaria vector mosquitoes*, second edition, 2016; check the titles and thresholds first): in the WHO tube test, mosquitoes exposed to a standard dose for an hour and scored 24 hours later, mortality of 98 percent or more is read as susceptible, 90 to 97 as possible resistance and below 90 as confirmed, so a net still protects as a barrier while its insecticide no longer kills, and the community-wide effect of nets (fewer mosquitoes surviving to bite anyone) fades first. Model it with invented numbers stated in the note: biting rates with and without nets, the fraction of mosquitoes killed per contact before and after resistance, and how the cases in the people who do not sleep under a net (the man who works nights, the older children) rise first. The reasonable no is the catechist, whose count is right and whose explanation is wrong; the failed first try is a second drive that hands out more of the same nets; the fix is the district's own entomology result, a switch to nets with a second active ingredient (piperonyl butoxide or a non-pyrethroid; check what WHO recommends and what the district stocks), and a count that measures bites rather than nets. Name no real programme's figures without a source, label every rate as the model's, and say in the note it is fiction, not medical or public-health advice. 'bed net' and 'pyrethroid' are unused in `overlap.mjs` (from `subjects.txt`).
- *(Done: The Sack That Read Dry, Makueni County, Kenya, 2049.)* *The sack that read dry*  (proposed 2026-11-20): a parish grain bank in a maize-growing district (pick a real region where aflatoxin in stored maize has been reported in published surveys, and check it) lends seed and food maize to members in the hungry months, and buys in only grain that its moisture meter passes. The meter is read on a handful from the top of each sack, in the afternoon, on the store's veranda, and the readings are all at or under the store's limit; in the third month the bottom layers of the stack smell musty and a district test finds aflatoxin above the legal limit. Work it before drafting, from named sources (for example the FAO's guidance on maize storage and the safe storage moisture for maize, often given as about 13 percent for long storage; the East African Standard for maize grain and its aflatoxin limits; and the published behaviour of *Aspergillus flavus*, which grows and makes aflatoxin in grain held warm and a few points too wet; check every threshold and title first): a capacitance meter reads grain moisture through its electrical properties, which change with temperature, so an uncorrected meter on sun-warmed grain can read wrong (find out which way, and by how much, for a stated meter, rather than guessing), and the top of a sack dries in the sun while its core does not. Model it with invented numbers stated in the note: moisture at the top and the core of a sack after a day in the sun, the meter's temperature error, and how many of a stack's sacks would fail a core reading taken cool, in the morning, with a probe. The reasonable no is the storekeeper, whose readings are honest and whose method is the one the meter's card shows; the failed first try is drying the whole stack again in the sun without changing how it is read; the fix is a morning reading from the core of a sample of sacks, a temperature-corrected meter, and a rule that a doubtful sack goes back to the drying floor, not into the stack. Name no real programme's figures without a source, label every reading as the model's, and say in the note it is fiction, not food-safety advice. 'grain moisture' and 'aflatoxin' are unused in `overlap.mjs` (from `subjects.txt`).
- *(Done: The Pump That Ate Itself, Ha Sekoati, Mokhotlong District, Lesotho, 2051; text only.)* *The pump that ate itself* (proposed 2026-11-20): a highland parish (pick a real town above about 1,800 m and check its altitude and air pressure) runs a surface centrifugal pump that lifts water from a well or river sump to a school tank, and its impeller wears out in months, pitted like a sponge, with a gravel-in-a-tin rattle the caretaker learns to hear. Work it before drafting, from named sources (a pump maker's published curves or a standard pump handbook, and steam tables for water's vapour pressure; check every title and figure first): a pump cavitates when the net positive suction head available (atmospheric pressure head, minus the suction lift, minus friction in the suction pipe, minus the water's vapour pressure head) falls below what the pump needs at its flow; air at altitude gives less head to start with, so a lift that works at the coast fails in the hills. Model it with invented numbers stated in the note (air pressure at the stated height, a suction lift of a few metres, pipe friction at two flows, a stated NPSH required), and show why the fix the committee wants, a bigger pump, makes it worse (more flow, more friction in the same suction pipe, a higher requirement). The reasonable no is the treasurer who has paid for two impellers and wants the bigger pump the dealer recommends; the failed first try is a new impeller of the same kind; the fix is setting the pump lower in a pit, a wider and shorter suction pipe, and a slower flow into the tank over more hours. Label every figure as the model's and say in the note it is fiction, not engineering advice. 'cavitation' and 'NPSH' are unused in `overlap.mjs` (from `subjects.txt`).
- *(Done: The Weld That Counted the Gusts, Brakdam, Hantam Karoo, Northern Cape, 2053; text only.)* *The weld that counted the gusts* (proposed 2026-11-20): a farm or mission in a dry, windy district (pick a real region where multi-blade wind pumps still lift water, such as the Karoo, the Namibian highlands or the Australian outback, and check it) has a wind pump whose pump rod or tail bracket cracks through a welded repair a few years after the local smith mended it, though the steel was never near its breaking load. Work it before drafting, from a named source (for example EN 1993-1-9, Eurocode 3 part 1-9 on fatigue of steel structures, with its detail categories, S-N curves and Palmgren-Miner damage sum, or a standard fatigue text; check the edition, the category numbers and the slope of the curve first): the stress range, not the peak stress, sets the life; each stroke or gust is a cycle; a weld toe is a notch that drops the allowable stress range for a given number of cycles a long way below plain steel, and doubling the stress range cuts the life by about eight on a slope-three curve. Model with invented numbers stated in the note: strokes per day from the wind's hours and the pump's speed, the stress range in the plain part and at the weld, and the damage sum year by year. The reasonable no is the smith, whose weld is sound and stronger than the bar; the failed first try is a thicker weld bead laid over the crack, which keeps the notch; the fix is a bolted or ground-smooth joint placed away from the bending, a brake set at a stated wind speed, and a crack check written into the calendar. Label every stress and life as the model's and say in the note it is fiction, not engineering advice. 'metal fatigue' and 'fatigue crack' are unused in `overlap.mjs` (from `subjects.txt`).
- *(Done: The Lake That Remembers the Fertiliser, Lannvoaz, Morbihan, 2026-11-21.)* *The lake that remembers the fertiliser*  (proposed 2026-11-21): a parish or village reservoir (pick a real farming region where summer blue-green algal blooms in small reservoirs are a known problem, and check it) greens every late summer, and two years after the farms upstream cut their fertiliser it still blooms. Work it before drafting, from named sources (for example the OECD's *Eutrophication of Waters: Monitoring, Assessment and Control*, 1982, and the Vollenweider phosphorus-loading model it built on, or a standard limnology text such as Wetzel's *Limnology*; check every title, edition and equation first): in most fresh waters phosphorus, not nitrogen, limits the bloom; the lake's phosphorus is the inflow load divided by the flushing and settling, so a stated load and residence time give a stated concentration; and phosphorus that settled into the mud over decades comes back out of it when the bottom water loses its oxygen in summer, so the lake keeps feeding itself for years after the inflow is cleaner. Model with invented numbers stated in the note: catchment load before and after the cut, the reservoir's volume and residence time, the predicted concentration from the load alone, and the internal load from the sediment that closes the gap with what is measured. The reasonable no is the farmer who did cut his fertiliser and wants to know why he bothered; the failed first try is dosing the bloom with an algaecide, which kills it in a week and lets its toxins out into the water at once; the fix is keeping the inflow cut, a buffer strip and a silt trap at the inlet, a measured honest timetable of several years, and a summer rule for the school's swimming and the cattle troughs. Keep clear of *The Grey Hour*, which already does a dawn oxygen crash in a fishpond: the turn here is the lake's memory, not the night. Label every figure as the model's and say in the note it is fiction, not water-management or health advice. 'eutrophication' and 'phosphorus' are unused in `overlap.mjs` (from `subjects.txt`).
- *(Done: The Stove That Saved Half, Gerba Mender (invented), Gurage highlands, Ethiopia, 31 Jan to 27 Sep 2051; text only.)* Direct rebound (Sorrell, UKERC 2007; Jevons, The Coal Question, 1865, backfire): per household per day 1.5 meals x 2.4 kg + 6 L x 0.15 + brewing 0.9 = 5.4 kg; stove halves each task, so 2.7 expected; measured 2.2 x 1.2 + 14 L x 0.075 + twice the brewing at half = 4.6 (167 to 142 kg in January, six loads to five); saving 0.81 of 2.7, rebound 70%; bean basket 2.0 to 0.8 kg every other day brings 4.0 (26% below); 60 houses need 87 t/yr, 2.5 ha eucalyptus coppice at an assumed 8 t/ha/yr gives 20 t (23%) from year six; walk about 21 h to 12 h a month; agent Mizan, Askale Gebre, Tsehay, Abba Tekle, Werknesh, Almaz; Meskel demera eve Wed 27 Sep.
- *(Done: The Road That Made It Later, Roccafonda, Marche, 2046.)* *The road that made it later* (proposed 2026-11-21): a hill town (pick a real region, for example the Apennines of the Marche or the Galician interior, and check it) opens a short link road between its two climbing routes so that pilgrims' cars to the shrine's September feast can switch from one to the other halfway up, and the feast-day crawl gets longer for everyone. Work it before drafting, from the named source (D. Braess, 'Über ein Paradoxon aus der Verkehrsplanung', *Unternehmensforschung* 12, 1968, and its English translation by Braess, Nagurney and Wakolbinger in *Transportation Science* 39, 2005; check both citations first): with each driver choosing the route quickest for them, the equilibrium with the new link can be slower for every driver than without it. - *(Done: The Ones Their Friends Name, Castlebay, Isle of Barra, 2051.)* *The ones their friends name* (proposed 2026-11-21): an island or valley parish (pick a real region and check it) gets fewer doses of a seasonal vaccine than it has people before an early winter wave, and its agent's first plan, doses to a random sample of the register, protects the network less than asking random people to name a friend and dosing the friend. Work it before drafting from the named sources (Scott L. Feld, 'Why your friends have more friends than you do', *American Journal of Sociology* 96, 1991; Reuven Cohen, Shlomo Havlin and Daniel ben-Avraham, 'Efficient immunization strategies for computer networks and populations', *Physical Review Letters* 91, 2003; Nicholas Christakis and James Fowler, 'Social network sensors for early detection of contagious outbreaks', *PLoS ONE*, 2010; check all three citations first): in a network with uneven numbers of contacts, a named friend has on average more contacts than a random person. Model with invented numbers stated in the note: a small contact network (households, the bar, the school bus, the choir), the mean contacts of a random person and of a named friend, and the share of contacts covered by each way of giving the same doses. The reasonable no is the nurse who says doses go first to the frail by rule, whatever a network says, and she is right: the story keeps her rule and uses the friend method only for the doses left over. The failed first try is the agent drawing friends from the register's own lists instead of asking people.
