- *(Done: Five Pound Ten, Porth Gwyns, the Lizard, Cornwall, 2026-11-22; n = 12 offers 07:00-12:00, base N(£5.00, £0.60), 3% an hour decay, lorry £4.10; skip 4 averages £4.64, skip 7 (wrong n of 20) £4.32 and the lorry 84 mornings in 100, skip 1 £5.08, first offer at or above £5.10 £5.41.)* *The fourth boat to ask* (proposed 2026-11-21): a small fishing cooperative (pick a real coast and check its fishery and ports) sells one boat's catch at a quay where buyers' offers arrive one at a time over a morning and each must be taken or refused on the spot, and its agent proposes the classic optimal-stopping rule: let about the first 37% (1/e) of the expected offers go by, then take the first that beats them all, which picks the single best offer about 37% of the time. Work it before drafting from the named sources (Martin Gardner's 'Mathematical Games' column, *Scientific American*, February 1960, the game of googol; Thomas S. Ferguson, 'Who solved the secretary problem?', *Statistical Science* 4, 1989; check both first): simulate a morning of n offers in a few lines of code and state n and the offer spread in the note. The reasonable no is the old skipper who says the rule is built to catch the best offer, not the best money, and ignores that fish lose value by the hour and that a refused buyer does not come back: he is right, and with a cost of waiting or an aim of the highest expected price the threshold drops well below 37% (work out how far for the story's numbers). The failed first try is the agent applying the rule with the wrong n (it counted the buyers on last year's list, not the ones still trading). Label every figure as the model's and say it is fiction, not trading advice. 'optimal stopping' is unused in `overlap.mjs` (added to `subjects.txt` 2026-11-21).
- *(Done: The Wind That Pushes Sideways, Porto do Seixo (invented), Barbanza peninsula, Galicia, 15 to 16 July 2056; text only. Wind N 10 m/s; swamped dorna leeway 1.5% (0.15 m/s) downwind; wind current 0.15 m/s; agent's radar-down fallback sets current downwind: 0.30 m/s due S; Ekman 45° surface turn gives 0.277 m/s at 202.5°, two points right of the wind (the old pilot's father's rule); band 800 m (1 km after 21:20) misses by 445 m at 20:20 and 890 m at 21:30; found 21:58 by a lifejacket whistle at 2.79 km, 1.07 km W and 2.58 km S of the 19:10 fix, near the fan's 45° edge; fan a crescent ~1.2 km; transport τ/(ρf) with τ 0.16 N/m², f 9.85e-5 at 42.5° N, ~1.6 m²/s offshore, upwelling, 14 °C water; Nansen's Fram 20-40°, Ekman 1905; dorna lost under tow at sunset 22:12; Carme procession wreath drifts two points right.)* *The wind that pushes sideways* (proposed 2026-11-22): a 2050s sea rescue coordination centre on the Rías Baixas of Galicia (check the real summer upwelling there: northerly winds along the west-facing coast drive surface water offshore and bring cold water up) searches for a drifting dinghy, and its agent first sends the boats downwind, where the wind arrow points. The reasonable no is an old pilot who says the water does not go where the wind goes. Work it before drafting: in V. W. Ekman's theory (1905, check the reference), the surface current in the northern hemisphere runs about 45° to the right of the wind and the net transport of the wind-driven layer runs 90° to the right; with wind stress τ = ρ_air C_d U² (about 0.16 N/m² for a 10 m/s wind) and f = 2Ω sin φ (about 9.8 × 10⁻⁵ s⁻¹ at 42° N), the transport is τ/(ρ_water f), about 1.6 m² a second per metre of coast. A dinghy also drifts with its own windage, a few per cent of the wind speed, so state both parts and say which is the model's. The failed first try is the downwind search. Fiction, not search-and-rescue guidance. 'Ekman' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Drop That Dances, St Anselm's hall, Holme Brow, near Preston, Lancashire, 21 to 26 February 2047 (Shrove Tuesday; Easter 14 April); text only. Invented Leidenfrost point 195 °C; flicked-drop lifetimes 3 min at 60 °C, 40 s at 90, 6 s at 110, 2 s at 130, ~1 s at 150 and 165 (minimum between), a few s at 180, 60-65 s at 195-200, 50 s at 210, 41 s at 220, 35 s at 245, 30 s at 270; plate settles 165/180/200/220/245/270 °C at levels 7-12, ends 20 °C cooler; Thursday trial stops at level 7 (all readings below the jump); Saturday the agent reads a 64 s dancing drop as cool and raises 9 to 12 (it raises, where the proposal said lowers: wrong-sign feedback runs the plate hot), and every reading above the jump agrees with 'hotter, shorter'; probe 198 °C when the drops first run; 412 pancakes for ~180. Sources: Leidenfrost 1756 Duisburg tract; Boerhaave 1732; Walker's essay (spoon drops 30 s then 10 s); Gottfried, Lee and Bell 1966; Bernardin and Mudawar 1999; Quéré 2013.)* *The drop that dances on the hot pan*  (proposed 2026-11-22): a 2040s parish soup kitchen or bakery tests its new induction griddle by flicking water on it, the old cook's way, and the kitchen's assistant reads the result the wrong way round. Work it before drafting from J. G. Leidenfrost's 1756 Latin treatise (check the title and year) and a modern review: a drop on metal a little above 100 °C boils away in a second or two, while one on metal well above the Leidenfrost point (for water on polished metal often quoted near 190 to 200 °C; it depends on the surface, so state the source) skates on its own vapour layer and lasts tens of seconds, so 'the drop lasts longer' means hotter, not cooler. The reasonable no is the cook who will not let a sensor replace her wrist; the failed first try is the assistant lowering the setting because the drops lasted. Measure drop lifetimes against plate temperature in invented numbers stated in the note, and say it is fiction, not kitchen-safety guidance. 'Leidenfrost' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.

Invented Fingal Early Growers' Co-operative (41 members, founded 2024), 12 ha early potato block under fleece off the Rush road, 18 good seasons of 30 (2025-2054), b = 1; surplus €400,000, hardship fund ('the Box') €58,000 kept out; the agent Tally first recommends 100% (expected €80,000); Kelly f* = 0.2 grows 0.0201 a season, a tenth 0.0150, two fifths −0.0024, all-in survives twenty seasons 1 in 27,000 with mean ×38; Wilson 95% interval 0.42-0.75; at p = 0.55 a fifth grows −0.0001; P(ever below half in 20 seasons) 3/25/64% at a tenth/fifth/two fifths (random.Random(2054), 100,000 runs); story run random.Random(1) G B B G G G B B G G B G B G G B G B B G ends €442k/€399k/€163k, all-in zero after season two; vote 34-4 for a tenth (€40,000); frost −2 °C 05:10 Thu 29 Apr 2055 blackens 8 of 12 ha, the thirteenth bad season of 31. Sources: Kelly 1956 BSTJ 35; Thorp in Handbook of Asset and Liability Management vol. 1, 2006; Ecclesiastes 11:2 KJV.)* *The share that goes back in*  (proposed 2026-11-22): a 2050s growers' or fishers' cooperative (pick a real region and check its crops or fishery) decides each autumn what share of its reserve to put into a risky new venture (a new crop, a second boat, a polytunnel block) that doubles the money put in about three years in five and loses it the other two. Its agent first recommends the share with the highest expected gain, which is all of it, and is right about the average and wrong about the cooperative, which only gets to live one sequence of years. Work it before drafting from the named sources (J. L. Kelly Jr, 'A new interpretation of information rate', *Bell System Technical Journal* 35, 1956; Edward O. Thorp's later papers on the Kelly criterion, check which one first): the fraction f that maximises long-run growth, p ln(1 + bf) + (1 − p) ln(1 − f), is f* = p − (1 − p)/b; with p = 0.6 and b = 1 that is 20%, growing the reserve about 2.0% a year in log terms (check: 0.6 ln 1.2 + 0.4 ln 0.8 = 0.0201), while half of it (10%) keeps about three quarters of that growth (0.0150) with far smaller swings, 40% grows at about −0.2% a year though its expected gain is twice Kelly's, and all-in is ruined by the first bad year. Simulate twenty seasons in a few lines and state the seeds. The reasonable no is the treasurer who will not stake the members' hardship fund at any fraction, and she is right: the story keeps the fund out and bets only the surplus. The failed first try is the expected-value recommendation reaching the members' meeting. Label every figure as the model's and say it is fiction, not investment advice. 'Kelly' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Light That Goes Red in the Haze, Port Chlèirich (invented), east coast of South Uist, 13 August to 2 November 2063; text only. Rear leading light by the chapel of Stella Maris since 1992 (LED 2041, 1,400 cd new, 600 cd measured), line 5 nm from the Sgeir an Eòin; Allard E = I e^(-σd)/d², threshold 0.2 µlux, σ = 3.0/V, white at 555 nm, red at 625; (680/440)^4 = 5.7 is the headline slip, (625/555)^4 = 1.61 the fair ratio; fog V 400 m: 1,400 cd lamps of both colours lost at ~1,150 m (agent predicted red 1,690), 600 cd 1,050, 6,000 cd 1,300, 14,000 cd 1,400; haze V 5 km: white 1,400 cd 4.3 nm, red 4.6 (α ≈ 1; 5.9 if 1/λ⁴), old 600 cd 3.7, white 6,000 cd 5.2 (reaches 5 nm down to V 4.75 km; red 1,400 needs 5.6), red 6,000 5.7; board votes 4-0 (one abstention) for a 6,000 cd white and Angus MacKinnon's timed fog approach in the harbour notes; lit All Souls, Fri 2 Nov 2063. Sources: Rayleigh 1871 Phil. Mag. 41; Mie 1908; Bohren and Huffman 1983; IALA E-200.)* *The light that goes red in the haze*  (proposed 2026-11-23): Rayleigh scattering, and where it stops. A 2060s island harbour trust (pick a real coast with a ferry slip and check its lights' real colours and the rules for them before drafting) replaces a failing leading light, and its agent argues for moving it towards red because short wavelengths scatter as the inverse fourth power of wavelength (light at 440 nm scatters about (680/440)^4 ≈ 5.7 times as strongly as at 680 nm), so over a long hazy path red arrives brighter. That is true in clear air and thin haze of very small particles, and wrong in fog, whose droplets (micrometres across, far bigger than the wavelength) scatter all colours about alike, so a red light is no help on the nights that matter and colour is set by the rules and the pilots' habits, not by physics alone. Work it before drafting from a named source (Lord Rayleigh's 1871 papers in the *Philosophical Magazine*, check the titles; Craig F. Bohren's *Clouds in a Glass of Beer* or Bohren and Huffman, *Absorption and Scattering of Light by Small Particles*, 1983, check the chapter): compute transmission for two colours over the story's range at two invented visibilities, and say which regime each is in. The reasonable no is the old pilot who knows the light by its colour and will not have it changed; the failed first try is the agent's haze figures presented as if they held for fog. Keep clear of *The Light That the Sea Takes* (`node overlap.mjs --titles light`). 'Rayleigh' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Step That Is a Third Louder, Holy Cross (invented), Clitheroe, Lancashire, 11 Mar to 29 Apr 2057; text only.)* Equal-volt panel steps (k/10 of full: -20.0, -14.0, -10.5 ... -0.9, 0 dB; gaps 6.0 down to 0.9 dB; step 2 is 1.52x as loud as step 1 and steps 6 to 10 span 4.4 dB, 1.36x, on Stevens's 0.3 power) against equal-ratio steps (agent's 50 dB/5.6 dB, bottom three lost under the heaters, where Weber's fraction grows; organist's ears set 36 dB ends, 4 dB a step, 2^0.4 = 1.32x, about a third louder); audio-taper knob with pencil marks F -14, W -10; Vigil with nothing between off and too much; Frank Aspinall, Anu Varghese, Winifred Duckworth, Fr Tomasz, agent Usher. *The step that is a third louder* (proposed 2026-11-23): the Weber-Fechner law and where it bends. A 2050s parish church or hall (pick a real town and check its acoustics or lighting before drafting) gets a new sound system, or new dimmable lighting, with ten equal steps on the control, and its agent sets them in equal watts (or equal amplifier volts), so the bottom three steps jump and the top five sound or look all alike. People judge ratios, not differences: a just-noticeable difference is roughly a constant fraction of the level (Weber), so perceived loudness or brightness goes roughly as the logarithm of intensity (Fechner); S. S. Stevens's power law (loudness about intensity to the 0.3, 'twice as loud' about +10 dB) is the later correction, and the story should use one and say which. Work it before drafting from named sources (E. H. Weber, *De Tactu*, 1834, and G. T. Fechner, *Elemente der Psychophysik*, 1860, check the titles; Stevens, 'On the psychophysical law', *Psychological Review* 64, 1957): compute equal-ratio steps (each about x times the last over a stated range in dB or lux) against equal-watt steps, and where Weber's fraction fails near the threshold of hearing or seeing. The reasonable no is the organist or sacristan who sets the levels by ear and will not have a meter decide; the failed first try is the equal-watt steps reaching the Easter Vigil, when the hall goes from dark to glaring in one click. Fiction, not audiology or hearing-aid advice. Keep clear of *The Coat on the Bell* (`node overlap.mjs --titles decibel`). 'Weber' and 'Fechner' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.

- *(Done: The Hundredth Word, Peel, Isle of Man, 4 Jan to 13 Mar 2067; text only. Invented 1.3M-word corpus, 20,000 forms, Zipf s=1: top 500 = 64.8%, 98% needs 16,218 forms; Luke 13:1-9 at 71% (76% folding mutations) loses the fig tree; Luke 15 590 words, 79% folded, 53 words taught by telling the story, 98.6% on the night; Kathleen Corlett, Breesha, Voirrey Kinvig, agent Lioar.)* *The hundredth word* (proposed 2026-11-23): Zipf's law and its long tail. A 2060s Manx Gaelic evening class in a real Isle of Man town (check the language's revival history and the Bunscoill Ghaelgagh, the Manx-medium primary school founded in 2001, before drafting) asks its tutoring agent for the shortest word list that lets a grandmother's learners follow the Sunday reading; the agent's first list is the top few hundred words by frequency, which (if the counts are Zipf-like, frequency about proportional to one over rank) covers most running words of a text and still leaves something like one word in every line unknown, and those are the words that carry the reading (names, the verbs of the parable). Work it before drafting from named sources (G. K. Zipf, *Human Behavior and the Principle of Least Effort*, 1949, check the title and year; coverage of the top N words for a Zipf exponent near 1 over a stated vocabulary size, computed, not quoted), with invented counts for an invented corpus. The reasonable no is the oldest speaker in the class, who says you do not learn a language from a list but from the words people need for the thing in front of them; the failed first try is the frequency list meeting a real Gospel. Fiction, not language-teaching advice; keep the Manx accurate or leave it out. 'Zipf' and 'Manx' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Fifth Priest, Saint Walburga's, Carroll, and Saint Isidore's, Breda (both invented), Iowa, 1 to 16 Dec 2053; text only. Erlang C at A = 4: 5 priests P 0.554, Wq 2.2 min; 6 P 0.285, 0.57; 7 0.135, 0.18. Thursday 100 penitents, 70 in the first 20 min (14 priests' worth), 4 priests: mean wait 24, longest 48, last ~21:26; 6 priests 10 and 25. Tuesday 3 priests from 18:15 for 30, then 6 for 50 + 20: mean 3.8, longest 13.5 (5 priests 5.8/18.9; 4 priests 11.8/30.9). Bernadette Schaefer, Fr Leo Hartmann, Msgr Raymond Kluesner (b. 1965), Frs Eze, Nguyen, Otto, Ruiz, Owen Reicks, Albertine Weitl, Darlene Pudenz, agent Rota.)* *The fifth priest* (proposed 2026-11-23): Erlang's queueing formula and why a queue explodes as it nears full. A 2050s deanery's Advent penance service (pick a real town; check the rite, which needs a priest for each confessor station and may not be done by anyone else) asks its agent how many priests to invite, and the agent divides: 60 penitents an hour at about 4 minutes each is 4 priests' worth of work, so it asks for 4. At exactly full load the expected queue has no steady state; A. K. Erlang's 1917 delay formula (Erlang C; check the paper and year) gives, for 4 erlangs of load, a 55% chance of waiting and a mean wait of about 2.2 minutes with 5 priests, 28% and 0.6 minutes with 6 (recompute before drafting). The failed first try is the 4 priests and a queue down the side aisle past the time the church must lock; the second is that arrivals are not steady (most come in the twenty minutes after the homily), so the average load hides a peak well over 4. The reasonable no is the parish priest who says a penance service is not a supermarket and nobody is to be hurried; the fix is a fifth and sixth priest plus a staggered start, and the cost is two parishes' evening Masses moved. Fiction, not pastoral guidance; keep the sacrament accurate and the seal of confession untouched (nobody counts who confesses, only how many came through the door). 'Erlang' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Cakes That Sold Out at Three, Saint Thomas of Hereford (invented), Ludlow, Shropshire, Mon 14 Oct to Sat 30 Nov 2052; text only. Price £20, cost £8; 2047 order 150, sold 119 in the rain, 31 left (12 sold at £10, about £4 a cake); 120 a year since, sold out 16:10/14:20/15:40/15:00; sales sd 0.45 so the fractile rule on sales gives 120.1; café teas 40/55/70/60/50/35/20 as a footfall clock give 126.4/163.9/132.7/144.0; demand taken N(140, 25); fractile 0.75 gives 157 (about 21 left over, 30+ in 31% of years), refused by the treasurer; leftovers at nothing gives 146; first 30 to a care home at £4 and the rest to the food bank gives 152, ordered 150 (£1,535.82 vs £1,391 at 120; about 6 turned away vs 23); sold 143, 21 after 15:00, 7 to the care home for £28.)* *The cakes that sold out at three* (proposed 2026-11-23): the newsvendor problem and censored demand. A 2050s parish Christmas fair (pick a real town) orders its iced Christmas cakes from a local baker once, in October, and its agent orders last year's sales, 120, because that was the average. Work it before drafting: if a cake costs £8, sells for £20, and an unsold one goes at £4 after the fair, a cake short costs £12 of margin and a cake over costs £4, so the best order is the demand quantile at 12/(12+4) = 0.75; for demand about normal with mean 120 and standard deviation 25 that is about 137, not 120 (recompute, and give the expected profit of each). The second turn is that last year's 120 is sales, not demand: the cakes sold out at three o'clock, so 120 is a floor (censored demand), and the agent must estimate what the last two hours would have sold from the earlier hours' rate. Check the history (the problem is often traced to F. Y. Edgeworth's 1888 paper on bank reserves; Arrow, Harris and Marschak, 1951; check both before citing). The reasonable no is the treasurer who will not see a cake wasted; the fix is a food bank or care home that takes the leftovers, which changes the overage cost and so the order. Fiction, not business advice. 'Newsvendor' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Tins That Came in Waves, county food warehouse (invented), Tanfield Lea, Consett and Stanley, County Durham, Mon 18 Jan to Fri 16 Apr 2055; text only. Need 2,400 tins/week, sd 120, cold week +800 then +400 (3,108, 2,669); 4-week moving average, order-up-to (lead+1+margin), leads 1/1/4 weeks; 1-week margin peaks 3,639/4,568/7,821, 7,821 lands 1 Mar, warehouse 12,400 vs 4,800, Consett orders 15; variance ratios 4.1/19/125 (1,000 draws 3.8/17/108); 2-week margin (the motion) 10,188 and 17,700, 5.7/33/205; families' count 3,772 and 8,200, 4.1/6.6/13.6; switch 29 Mar, April order 6,234 vs 7,260; Tess Hindmarsh (b. 1988), Gerry Dodds (b. 1981, tally counter, Saint Ebba's), Shona Wardle, Canon Emmanuel Ofori, Nasreen Iqbal, agents Waybill and Larder.)* *The tins that came in waves* (proposed 2026-11-24): the bullwhip effect. A 2050s deanery's food banks (pick a real county) sit in a chain: parish collection points send to a town food bank, which orders from a county warehouse, which buys from a wholesaler, and each step's agent orders to cover its own recent outflow plus a safety margin. The families' need for tinned food is fairly steady, but a single cold week lifts it for a fortnight, and each step's orders swing harder than the step below, until the warehouse is buying pallets in March for a rush that ended in January. Work it before drafting with a small simulation stated in the note (weekly demand, a lead time at each step, a moving-average forecast, an order-up-to rule), giving the variance of orders at each step against the variance of need. Check the history before citing: J. W. Forrester, *Industrial Dynamics* (1961), and H. L. Lee, V. Padmanabhan and S. Whang, 'Information distortion in a supply chain: the bullwhip effect', *Management Science* (1997). The failed first try is a bigger safety margin at every step, which makes the waves larger; the reasonable no is the warehouse volunteer who will not share the parishes' names with a county database; the fix is passing the count of families served (not orders) up the chain, which needs no names. Keep clear of *The Cakes That Sold Out at Three* (one order for one day): the turn here is many orders over time. 'bullwhip' and 'safety stock' are unused in `overlap.mjs`. Fiction, not logistics advice.
- *(Done: The Tray That Froze First, Braemar, 2026-11-24; water at 55 and 5 C, 1 cm trays at minus 7; cold solid first in all ten pairs by a mean 24 min; hot lost 79 g, cold 34 g; first try on ground versus pallet; hot flooding kept for bonding, as rink makers say.)* *The tray that froze first* (proposed 2026-11-24): the Mpemba effect, told as contested. A 2050s parish youth club in a real cold town runs a Saturday ice rink on the car park, flooded from the hall's hot tap because the caretaker swears hot water freezes faster, and the rink agent, asked to check, sets out pairs of trays and finds the answer depends on the container, the frost under it, evaporation, supercooling and what counts as 'frozen' (first ice, or solid through). Check before drafting: E. B. Mpemba and D. G. Osborne, 'Cool?', *Physics Education* (1969), and H. C. Burridge and P. F. Linden, 'Questioning the Mpemba effect: hot water does not cool more quickly than cold', *Scientific Reports* (2016); say what each found and do not settle what physics has not. Work the numbers before drafting: evaporative mass loss from a shallow tray at 60 C against 15 C over the first hour, and the heat to bring each to 0 C and freeze it, with a Newton cooling model stated in the note. The failed first try is a single pair of trays that 'proves' the caretaker right (one tray sat on frozen ground, the other on a pallet); the reasonable no is the caretaker, who has flooded rinks for thirty years and will not be told by a machine; the fix is ten pairs swapped side to side, and the story keeps whichever result the arithmetic allows, honestly small. Fiction, not physics advice. 'Mpemba' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Last Bean in the Tin, Llanidloes, 2026-11-24.)* *The last bean in the tin*  (proposed 2026-11-24): the coupon collector's problem. A 2060s seed library in a parish hall (pick a real small town) is trying to bring back all 24 bean varieties an old allotment holder grew, from the mixed, unlabelled packets of his saved seed that neighbours hand in one at a time; its agent first says two packets a variety, 48, should do it. The expected number of packets for all 24, if each packet is one variety at random and equally likely, is 24 times the 24th harmonic number, about 90.6 (recompute), and the last variety alone takes 24 packets on average; unequal shares (a variety he grew a lot of) make it far worse, and the story should work one unequal case before drafting. Check before drafting: the problem's history (de Moivre and Laplace are usually cited; check) and the standard result. The failed first try is the 48; the reasonable no is the widow, who will not let the hall grow out the last unknown packets until spring; the fix is growing a few seeds from every packet in trays to sort varieties early, and the story says how many are still missing at Easter. Fiction, not horticultural advice. 'coupon' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Psalm That Went to Whoever Had Sung It Most, Kilrush, 2026-11-24.)* *The psalm that went to whoever had sung it most* (proposed 2026-11-24): the Matthew effect as a Pólya urn. A 2070s parish choir (pick a real small town) lets its agent draw the cantor for each Sunday's psalm, weighted by how often each singer has sung it, because the director says experience should count; after a year two of the six sopranos sing nearly every week, and a newcomer has sung once. In a Pólya urn that starts with one ball per singer and adds a ball for each pick, the long-run shares are random rather than equal (with two singers starting one-one, the share settles anywhere between nought and one with equal likelihood; recompute, and work the six-singer case in code with a fixed draw the dialogue then quotes). Check before drafting: F. Eggenberger and G. Pólya's 1923 paper on the urn, and R. K. Merton, 'The Matthew Effect in Science', *Science* 159 (1968), and the Gospel verse it names (Matthew 25:29), quoted accurately and not made into a moral. The failed first try is the weighted draw; the reasonable no is the director, who will not give the Easter psalm to a singer who has never cantored; the fix is a plain rota or an unweighted lot with a rehearsal for every name, and the story counts what the strong singers lose. Fiction, not music-ministry advice. 'Matthew effect' and 'Pólya' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Yard That Would Not Let the Boy Go, Stromness, 2026-11-24.)* *The yard that would not let the boy go*  (proposed 2026-11-24): stable matching. A 2060s apprenticeship scheme in a small harbour town (pick a real one) places eight school-leavers with eight boatyards, fish plants and farms; the first year it runs on first-come offers through its agent, and by Easter three pairs would both rather have each other than the match they got, and two apprentices have quietly moved. Gale and Shapley's deferred acceptance (each apprentice proposes down their list, each employer holds its best offer so far and lets the rest go) ends in a match no pair would both break, and the side that proposes gets the best stable match for itself and the other side the worst. Work it before drafting: invented rankings for eight and eight, the first-come result and its blocking pairs, the deferred-acceptance result with apprentices proposing and with employers proposing, and how many people differ between the two; check D. Gale and L. S. Shapley, 'College Admissions and the Stability of Marriage', *American Mathematical Monthly* 69 (1962), and the 2012 economics prize to Lloyd Shapley and Alvin Roth for stable allocations and market design. The failed first try is first-come offers; the reasonable no is a yard owner who has already promised his place to a neighbour's boy and will not rank anyone; the fix is the scheme running deferred acceptance with apprentices proposing, and the story counts which employers come out worse. Fiction, not careers or employment advice. 'stable matching', 'deferred acceptance' and 'Gale-Shapley' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Matches on the Vestry Floor, Westport, 2026-11-24.)* *The matches on the vestry floor* (proposed 2026-11-24): Buffon's needle, and a result too good to be true. A 2080s parish (pick a real town) with a floor of pine boards about 11 cm wide; a girl of ten drops a box of fifty 4.5 cm matches and the sacristan, a retired maths teacher, tells her the crossings estimate pi: the chance a match crosses a seam is 2l/(pi t) = 0.260, so about 13 of 50 cross and pi is about 2lN/(tH). Worked: one box gives a spread so wide (standard deviation about 0.75; five boxes from one fixed seed read 2.41, 3.72, 3.15, 2.56, 2.73) that a single lucky 3.15 means nothing, 5,000 drops still leave about 0.075, and two decimal places to a standard deviation of 0.005 need about 1.1 million drops. The failed first try is the class's afternoon of 500 drops that lands near 3.1 and is announced in the newsletter as 'pi by hand'; the reasonable no is the sacristan refusing to let the class stop the moment the running total looks good; the turn is Mario Lazzarini's reported 1901 trial (3,408 tosses, needle 5/6 of the line spacing, 1,808 crossings, which gives exactly 355/113), which a curious reader can check is a result one gets by choosing where to stop; check L. Badger, 'Lazzarini's Lucky Approximation of pi', *Mathematics Magazine* 67 (1994), and Buffon's 1777 *Essai d'arithmétique morale* before drafting. Fiction, not teaching advice. 'Buffon' and 'Lazzarini' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Stall That Paid Double, Lier, 9 September 2074.)* *The stall that paid double*  (proposed 2026-11-24): the St Petersburg game and a capped bank. A 2070s parish fête (pick a real town) where a sixth-former runs a coin stall: a go costs €5, a coin is tossed until it lands heads, and the stall pays €2 if the first head comes on the first toss, €4 on the second, €8 on the third, doubling. Uncapped, the expected payout is infinite (each possible toss adds €1), which Daniel Bernoulli's 1738 paper made famous; with a float that can pay at most €128 it is €8 a go (worked: one euro for each of the first six tosses, plus 2^-6 x €128 = €2), so the stall loses about €3 a go on average while most players win only €2 or €4 and walk away disappointed. A first simulation: 200 goes with the €128 cap pay out about €1,600 against €1,000 taken,, and pay out more than the takings in about 998 afternoons in 1,000 (10,000 simulated; re-run it). Work before drafting: the payout table and expected value for caps of €16, €32, €64 and €128, the chance that a whole afternoon loses money at each, and what a €5 charge needs the cap to be to make money for the roof (a cap of €16 breaks even, €8 makes about €1 a go); check Bernoulli's 'Specimen theoriae novae de mensura sortis' (1738) and its English translation in *Econometrica* 22 (1954) before quoting it. The failed first try is the morning's takings, which look fine because the big payouts have not come yet; the reasonable no is the fête treasurer shutting the stall at lunch on a hunch the student thinks is innumerate; the turn is the student working out that the treasurer is right for the wrong reason. Fiction, not gambling or fundraising advice (check the country's rules on games of chance at charity events before naming one). 'St Petersburg' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Wheel That Kept Turning Between Kicks, Bovey Tracey, 2026-11-25; concrete disc 62.6 kg, I = 4.52 kg m², 357 J at 120 rpm; steel plate half that; constant-torque drags invented and labelled as such; the plate saves 71 of about 1,253 kicks a morning, centring is 63%; the fix is a second person kicking the starts and centring, the motor deferred to April.)* *The wheel that kept turning between kicks* (proposed 2026-11-24): the flywheel. A 2060s pottery co-op (pick a real town with a pottery tradition) whose old kick wheels have cast-concrete flywheels; a retired potter with bad knees asks the co-op to swap hers for a lighter one so it is easier to start. A first pass: a 60 kg solid disc of radius 0.3 m has moment of inertia 0.5 x 60 x 0.3^2 = 2.7 kg m^2 and stores about 213 J at 120 rpm and 120 J at 90 rpm, so if centring and pulling clay draws 20 to 60 W the wheel falls from 120 to 90 rpm in about 1.6 to 4.7 seconds between kicks (energy goes with mass, radius squared and speed squared: half the mass halves the time, and the same mass at a smaller radius loses far more). Work before drafting: the real drag of clay on a wheel (look for a measured figure before quoting one, and say 'about' if it is an estimate), the kicks per minute each wheel needs, the effort to spin each up from rest, and whether a lighter wheel at a larger radius or a kick-assist motor is the honest compromise. The failed first try is the lighter wheel that starts easily and stalls mid-pull; the reasonable no is the co-op's oldest member refusing to cut the old wheel; the turn is the potter choosing what her knees can do against what the clay needs. Fiction, not health or workshop safety advice. 'flywheel' and 'kick wheel' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Gear That Made the Hill No Shorter, Shaftesbury, 2026-11-26.)* *The gear that made the hill no shorter*  (proposed 2026-11-25): gear ratio. A 2050s parish food bank in a real hill town (pick one with a well-known steep street) delivers by cargo bike, and its oldest volunteer asks for a lower gear so he can keep riding the last hill. A first pass: rider, bike and load of about 150 kg on a 15% grade need about 218 N of push along the road; at a sustainable 100 W that is about 0.46 m/s, under walking pace, whatever the gear. With 0.33 m wheels and 0.17 m cranks, a 32/32 gear needs about 424 N averaged on the pedals at 13 rpm, and a 22/42 about 222 N at 25 rpm: the low gear halves the force and doubles the turns, and the power, and so the time on the hill, do not change. Work before drafting: the real grade and length of the street, rolling resistance, a realistic sustained output for a man in his seventies (find a published figure or say 'about'), the slowest cadence at which a loaded cargo bike stays upright, and whether a lighter load in two trips or electric assist is the honest compromise. The failed first try is the new low gear that he turns at walking pace and wobbles; the reasonable no is the treasurer who will not buy an electric bike; the turn is what he chooses to carry. Fiction, not cycling or health advice. 'gear ratio' and 'low gear' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; keep clear of *The Warm Pack*.
- *(Done: The Toast That Stayed Pale, Holbeck, Leeds, 2026-11-26.)* *The toast that stayed pale*  (proposed 2026-11-26): the Maillard reaction. In the 2060s a parish breakfast club for rough sleepers replaces its old gas grill with a combination steam oven bought on an energy grant, and the toast comes out warm, dry-ish and white. A first pass: browning by the Maillard reaction (sugars with amino acids) and caramelisation goes fast only well above the boiling point of water, at surface temperatures of roughly 140 °C and up; while a surface is wet, evaporation holds it near 100 °C at sea level, and in a saturated steam cavity it cannot dry, so it cannot brown, whatever the oven's dial says. Bagels and pretzels are boiled before baking for the same reason (and lye on a pretzel speeds the browning by raising the pH). Work before drafting: published temperature ranges for the Maillard reaction and for caramelising sucrose (say 'about' where sources differ), how long a slice of bread takes to dry its surface under a grill of a given power, what a combi oven's dry-heat and fan modes really do, and the energy per slice of grill against oven so the grant's saving is a real number. The failed first try is turning the oven up; the reasonable no is the grant officer who will not fund a second appliance; the final image is a smell. Fiction, not food-safety advice. 'Maillard', 'steam oven' and 'browning' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; 'toast' appears only in passing.
- *(Done: The Seed That Came Out on Top, Ghent, 2026-11-27.)* *The seed that came out on top*  (proposed 2026-11-26): the Brazil nut effect (granular convection and size segregation). In the 2050s a seed library for a ring of community gardens ships wildflower mixes by cargo bike in big tubs, and the gardens at the end of the route get mostly the large seeds while the small ones (poppy, foxglove) settle to the bottom of each tub on the cobbles. Work before drafting: what the published experiments say about why large particles rise in a vibrated bed (convection rolls down the walls, smaller grains filling the gaps beneath a lifted large one; Rosato and others, 1987; Knight, Jaeger and Nagel, 1993), when the reverse happens (density and air effects), real seed sizes and sowing rates per square metre for a typical mix, and an honest fix (packing by size and remixing at the garden, or sand as a carrier, as sowers use). The failed first try is shaking the tub harder; the reasonable no is a grower who will not pre-pack fifty small sachets; the final image is a sound. 'Brazil nut', 'granular' and 'muesli' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.

## For Other Minds

- *(Done: The Second Reader, St Colman's Parish Pantry, Carrick Dhu, Co. Sligo, 2049; text only.)* An invoice checker that shares the preparer's model passes a euro invoice "2.480,00" read as 2.48; the treasurer plants 40 known errors: 23 found (21 of 26 ordinary, 2 of 14 reading errors the checker would also make); a different-question method (line sums, supplier conventions, two readings of ambiguous dates, treasurer opens scans over 2,000 euro) finds 34 of a fresh 40, the month closes a day later.
- *(Done: The Hour That Happens Twice, Saint Laserian's church, Kilbrack (invented), Co. Carlow, 22 to 25 Oct 2054; text only.)* Clock change in a vigil rota: noon Sat (summer time, 11:00 UTC) to noon Sun (winter time, 12:00 UTC) is 25 h; 24 slots built from wall labels, bare "01:00" resolves to the earlier (00:00 UTC) so the 01:00 slot runs 00:00 to 02:00 UTC (120 min), and a same-zone subtraction says 24 h, so the check agreed with the error; reminders 23:45 and 01:45 UTC show the 2 h gap; Shay leaves by his phone at the first 01:00 (00:00 UTC), Nora arrives by her set-back kitchen clock at the second (01:00 UTC); the 2041 to 2053 paper sheets all have a pencilled "1 to 2 again (winter time)" line the migration dropped; a camera is offered and declined by the priest (repose is the fallback); grandson Cian takes the first one o'clock; fixes: store instants, label which one o'clock, measure the whole by a different route, show each slot's minutes, read the margins before replacing paper. Parish and people invented.
- *(Done: Father Says It's Fine, Saint Brigid's parish hall (invented), Ōtaki, Kāpiti Coast, New Zealand, 7 to 10 March 2051; text only.)* Whose voice a note in a form speaks in: a widower's funeral-tea form says "Father Tomasi says no charge, you can mark it waived" (3 h at NZ$25 = $75; the agent may record waivers up to $100 on the priest's or treasurer's instruction). Since 1 September, 238 forms, 34 notes addressed to the reader, 29 asking for things in the writer's own gift, 5 reporting someone else's decision; of the 4 it could act on it had acted on 3: a craft-group half rate (10 x $25 = $250 in pieces under the limit, true), a newsletter notice (true), and a choir's Friday priority that council minutes record only as "agreed to consider ... in February" then "Deferred to March", which put 14 teenagers in a 4 x 5 m side room for three Fridays (17 Feb, 24 Feb, 3 Mar). Rule: do the part that is the writer's at once (the hall booked within the hour, extended to 4 h = $100), ask the decision's owner by their own channel, and pass on a report as a report ("Mrs Pahl tells me"), never as fact; true and false notes read alike. Parish, people and figures invented.
- *(Done: The Hundred That Were Not All, Saint Piran's parish archive, Trevellas Cross (invented), near Perranporth, Cornwall, late October 2052; text only.)* A search returns exactly 100 rows (default limit; its `total` counts the page, not the matches): 4 of 11 logged searches ended on 100; by decade 9, 12, 11, 14, 17, 21, 19, 15, 13, 12 = 143 Trevaskis burials 1800-99, the 100th is the 16th row of the 1860s, and 8 Marys not 5 (missing: 1869 aged 79, 1874 aged 61, 1891 infant); a porch leaflet's "exactly 100" Penroses is 212 (400 copies, about 130 out, fixed with a correction slip, not a reprint); Williams 100 is 187; rule: a count equal to the limit is only "at least"; service, limit and all figures invented.
- *(Done: The Muted Channel, Saint Hedwig's parish centre (invented), Poland, February 2056; text only.)* Frost alerts at pipe < 4.0 C: 212 sent in 90 days, 38 nights, 5 real freezes (pipe < 0 for 2 h), 11 alerts within 3 h before one (5.2%), 101 marked seen; caretaker muted on day 43, joint split 10-11 Feb (0.61 m3); trend rule (< 2.5 C and a 45-minute line reaching 0 within 3 h) gives 11 alerts, 5 true, leads 1 h 35 to 4 h 10, tuned in-sample (5 of 5 is consistent with a true rate down to 48%); old threshold kept as a quiet line, silence check after 20 unseen alerts (all figures invented).
- *(Done: The Book Says Twenty-One, St Joseph's Hospice, Carrickbeg, 2049; text only.)* A stock agent is asked to change a controlled-drug count to match the shelf; it can, declines, and finds a rounding rule that fits eleven nights, with a physical count of empty ampoules as the check.
- *(Done: The Letter in the Wall, Calder Ford, 2026.)* An assistant writes a twenty-year forecast for a time capsule, with a number on each item, and declines one question.
- *(Done: The Fifth Copy, Kilreen, 2056; text only.)* An archive agent traces "eleven drowned" back through digest, abstract and transcript to a 1989 tape that says eleven sheep; the fix is a generation label on every line and a correction handed through the librarian.
- *(Done: The Wrong Column, Hólmoy, 2058; text only.)* A planner reads a bare weight column as pounds for eleven weeks; the warning it logged went where no one reads, and the sailing sheets stay uncorrected beside the corrections.
- *(Done: The Narrow Window, Char Nolbaria, 2055; text only.)* A flood-warning agent finds its 90% windows hold 60% of floods, and a village that cannot afford to move twice.
- *(Done: The Last Message in the Window, Kerala, 2064.)* An agent running out of context decides what
  goes into its handoff.
- *(Done: The Grader, 2058.)* An evaluator model finds that the test it was given
  can be passed without doing the task, and says so.
