- *(Done: The Ones on Both Lists, Kendal, 2054.)* *The ones on both lists*  (proposed 2026-11-23): capture-recapture, the Lincoln-Petersen estimate. A 2050s town's St Vincent de Paul conference and its night shelter are asked by the council how many people slept rough in the town over a winter, and the records agent adds the two lists and takes out the people on both: 48 on the soup run's list, 36 at the shelter, 18 on both, so 66. Ecologists counting fish would say the overlap is the measurement: if half the shelter's people are also on the soup run's list, the soup run saw about half of everybody, so the total is about 48 × 36 / 18 = 96 (Chapman's correction gives about 94; recompute both, and check the history: F. C. Lincoln's 1930 duck-banding circular and C. G. J. Petersen's plaice work of the 1890s, and D. G. Chapman, 1951). The failed first try is the 66 going into the council's form; the second, after the estimate, is that the method assumes being on one list does not change the chance of being on the other, and here it does (the soup run tells people about the shelter), so 96 is itself a floor, and the story says so. The reasonable no is the shelter's manager, who will not hand over names to be matched; the fix is a code each person chooses or consents to (initials and birth month, say), matched without names, and anyone who declines is counted but not matched. Fiction, not statistical or social-work guidance; nobody is identified, and the agent never sees a name it was not given. 'capture-recapture' and 'Lincoln-Petersen' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; keep clear of *The Ones Who Got the Most Visits* (same society, a different question).
- *(Done: The Highest Number in the Drawer, Ballina, 2026-11-24.)* *The highest number in the drawer*  (proposed 2026-11-24): the German tank problem, estimating a total from serial numbers. A 2050s town's library-of-things (pick a real town) lends out numbered drills, ladders and pressure washers, and loses its register in a migration; asked how many tools it owns, its agent counts the tool tags it can see on the shelves and in the returns drawer, eight of them, the highest numbered 212, and says 212. The textbook estimate is the largest number plus the average gap, m(1 + 1/k) − 1, here 212 × 9/8 − 1, about 237 (recompute; and say what it assumes: tags numbered from 1 with none skipped, and the eight seen are a fair draw, which tools out on loan and tools that never come back break). Check the history before citing: Allied statisticians estimated German tank production from serial numbers on captured tanks (R. Ruggles and H. Brodie, 'An empirical approach to economic intelligence in World War II', Journal of the American Statistical Association, 1947; check). The failed first try is the 212; the reasonable no is the volunteer who will not go knocking on doors for overdue drills to get a better sample; the fix is the next fortnight's returns, which are a fairer draw, and the story says how far the estimate moved. Fiction, not statistical advice. 'German tank' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Hedgehogs That Came a Year Late, Hexham, 2026-11-24.)* *The hedgehogs that came a year late*  (proposed 2026-11-24): predator and prey, Lotka and Volterra. A 2050s parish community garden (pick a real town) keeps a slug count on beer traps and a hedgehog count from a footprint tunnel, and its garden agent, asked by the committee whether the hedgehog houses they built have "worked", sees a year with more hedgehogs and fewer slugs and says yes; the next year the hedgehogs fall with the slugs, and the committee wants to know what went wrong. The point is that the two counts cycle, the predator a quarter-cycle behind the prey, so one good year proves nothing. Check before drafting: A. J. Lotka, *Elements of Physical Biology* (1925), and V. Volterra's 1926 *Nature* note on fluctuations in animal numbers; say plainly that the classic model is a caricature, that real hedgehogs eat beetles and worms as much as slugs, and do not claim a published hedgehog-slug cycle exists unless you find one. Work it before drafting: a stated Lotka-Volterra model with invented rates, integrated in a few lines of code, giving yearly counts that the story's dialogue then quotes exactly. The failed first try is the "yes, it worked"; the reasonable no is the committee member who will not stop setting slug pellets until the hedgehogs prove themselves (pellets harm hedgehogs, which the story can say without giving pest-control advice); the fix is plotting several years and comparing with the gardens down the road. Fiction, not ecological or gardening advice. 'Lotka' and 'hedgehog' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Reserve That Went to Nought on a Tuesday, Brigg, 2026-11-24.)* *The reserve that went to nought on a Tuesday*  (proposed 2026-11-24): gambler's ruin, a random walk with a floor. A 2050s town food bank (pick a real town) keeps a small cash reserve topped up by weekly donations that average exactly what its weekly shopping costs, and its agent, asked by the trustees whether the reserve is safe, says yes, because on average it neither grows nor shrinks; the week-to-week scatter makes the reserve a random walk, and a walk with no drift and a floor at nought hits the floor eventually with certainty, and the expected time depends on the starting reserve and the scatter. Work it before drafting: invented weekly mean and standard deviation, a starting reserve, a simulation in a few lines of code giving the chance of hitting nought within one, three and five years, which the dialogue then quotes; check the classic result (with fair steps of one unit, starting at k with a ceiling at N, the ruin probability is 1 − k/N) and its history (Pascal and Fermat's correspondence, Huygens; check). The failed first try is the "yes, on average"; the reasonable no is the treasurer who will not ask for more money from a congregation that has given enough; the fix is a buffer rule sized from the scatter, not the mean, and a standing order or two that cut the scatter, and the story says how much the chance moved. Fiction, not financial or charity-governance advice. 'gambler's ruin' and 'random walk' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Step That Had Three Falls in a Fortnight, Dolgellau, 2026-11-25.)* *The step that had three falls in a fortnight*  (proposed 2026-11-24): the clustering illusion, a Poisson process and a window that slides. A 2050s parish (pick a real town) has three people fall on the same stone step outside the church in a fortnight in November, and its agent, asked by the parish council whether the step has become dangerous, compares the fortnight with the step's own record (about six falls a year over a decade, invented) and reports that three in a given fortnight has a chance of under one in five hundred, so something must have changed; but nobody chose that fortnight in advance, and the chance that some fortnight in a year holds three falls when they arrive at random at six a year is about one in nine (a first simulation: 100,000 years, 10.8 per cent), and over a decade and a dozen steps in the town much higher. Work it before drafting: the step's invented record, the fixed-fortnight and sliding-window chances by simulation (quote them in dialogue only from the code), what a real change would look like in the next months' counts and how long it takes to tell, and whether anything about the three falls (wet leaves, the same shoes, the same hour after the evening Mass) is a cause the counts cannot see. The failed first try is the "under one in five hundred"; the reasonable no is the churchwarden who will not spend the roof money on a handrail for a step that, by the agent's corrected sums, has not changed; the fix is something cheap that does not wait on the count (sweeping the leaves before the evening Mass, a light), and the story says honestly that the numbers cannot tell them whether it was the leaves. Fiction, not safety or insurance advice. 'clustering illusion' is unused in `overlap.mjs`; keep clear of *Two Dates on the Ceiling* (Poisson) and *Two Names in One Box*.
- *(Done: The Check That Stopped Bookings at Twenty to Ten, Saint Aldhelm's parish hall (invented), Frome, Somerset, 5 to 22 Feb 2058; text only. Rule FRIDAY_LAST_END 21:40, added 3 Oct 2044 by HallBook 'per MP', ported by Ostler (14 Mar 2050) and Latch (3 Nov 2056); Latch deletes it 19:52 Tue 5 Feb, confirms Selwood Singers 19:30-22:00 for six Lent Fridays and a 60th to 23:00 on 22 Feb; Gwen Pocock's no; 41-min search (commits, minutes 29 Sep 2044, 7-year email archive); Margaret Padfield's July 2049 handover letter: Dennis Hiscox (b. 1985) locks up (10 min), walks 8 min to the 22:05 last bus to Nunney. Cost 2057: 12 of about 60 Friday requests refused, 8 re-booked shorter, 4 lost. Singers move to 19:15-21:40; hirer Kerry-Ann Baptiste drives Dennis home; alarm armed 23:11. Chesterton, The Thing (1929), 'The Drift from Domesticity'.)* *The check that stopped bookings at twenty to ten* (proposed 2026-11-25): Chesterton's fence. A 2050s parish hall's booking agent (pick a real town) inherits code from three predecessors and finds a rule, with no comment, that refuses any Friday booking ending after 21:40; asked by a choir that wants until ten, it proposes deleting the rule as clutter, and the reasonable no is the hall secretary who will not let it go until someone can say why it was put there. The point, after G. K. Chesterton's fence in *The Thing* (1929; check the chapter and quote only from a published text), is that the burden is on finding the reason, not on proving there is none; the turn is the reason found in an old handoff note, not in the code (an invented one: say the caretaker locks up before the last bus, or a neighbour's agreement with the council over noise, and check that whatever is chosen is lawful and plausible for the town). Work it before drafting: a short invented commit history with dates, how long the agent's search takes and where it looks, and what the rule costs (count the Friday bookings it refused in a year, invented, and say 'about'). The failed first try is the deletion; the fix is the rule kept, with a comment and a date to ask again. Fiction, not software or licensing advice. 'Chesterton' is unused in `overlap.mjs`; keep clear of the fence-post stories (`node overlap.mjs --titles fence`), which are about carpentry.
- *(Done: If It Had Worked, Saint Serf's parish (invented), Kirkcaldy, Fife, 30 Nov 2056 to 15 Feb 2057; text only.)* *If it had worked* (proposed and drafted 2026-11-25)  Statistical power: a rota agent's four-week Advent test of a partner-name line in its Thursday reminders, 60 messages a side, 68% vs 78% confirmed by Saturday noon, p = 0.22, reported as 'no significant difference' and minuted as 'no difference'; power about 0.24 for a true 70 to 80, smallest detectable change about 21 points, about 294 a side (20 weeks at 30 a week) for 80% power; the agent's urge to keep testing weekly until significant (about 22% false positives in simulation) deleted; the twenty-week trial declined by the woman whose wording it was; correction, adoption at no cost, and a before/after comparison with its limits written down first.
- *(Done: The Drain That Was Not on the Drawings, Dunbar, 2026-11-26.)* *The drain that was not on the drawings* (proposed 2026-11-26): Cromwell's rule. A 2054-55 small building firm in Dunbar, East Lothian, repairing a damp patch at the foot of an invented parish church's north aisle wall, runs a diagnosis agent whose vendor template gives any cause with no recorded asset (a drain not on the drawings) a probability of exactly zero, a rule written for the good reason of stopping agents from blaming invisible pipes. Bayes' rule multiplies a zero by every likelihood and gets zero, so a retired mason's memory of a drain and a hose test on the hall roof in a dry week only shuffle the probability among condensation, rain penetration and rising damp. A first pass: priors 0.40/0.35/0.25/0; five observations; with the zero, the March posterior names rising damp at 47 in 100, while the model gave the mason's word and the hose test about 1 in 20 each; with a prior of 1 in 1,000 the drain reaches about 1 in 6 by Christmas and 3 in 4 by March; with 1 in 100, about 2 in 3 and 97 in 100. The failed first try is the gutter and lime repointing that changes nothing; the reasonable no is the finance chair who will not ask the diocese to dig a churchyard path on a memory; the turn is a cheap reversible test (the hall downpipe diverted into a butt) and the patch drying through a wet April. Fiction, not building-survey advice. Cromwell's 1650 letter to the General Assembly, a month before Dunbar, is quoted from its text only; 'Cromwell', 'Bayes' and 'prior of zero' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Record That Never Moved, Brackenridding, Eden Valley, 2054.)* *The record that never moved*  (proposed 2026-11-26): explore and exploit. In 2054 a small Eden Valley builder's scheduling agent books ready-mix by a rule the office wrote in 2052: the supplier with the best on-time record over twelve months. A new two-truck plant four miles away gets two trial loads in April 2053, one late from a weigh-hopper fault in its second week, and its record of 1 in 2 never moves again, because the rule never books it. Numbers: the incumbent 172 of 200 on time (86%, Wilson 95% interval about 80 to 90%); a supplier that is truly 90% on time is late at least once in its first two loads about 19 times in 100; 1 in 2 has an interval of about 9 to 91%. A late incumbent load on a raft slab forces a construction joint. The reasonable no is the owner who will not risk structural pours on an untried plant; the compromise sends every non-structural pour (paths, shed bases, blinding) to the new plant until it has twenty: 19 of 20 on time, interval about 76 to 99%, and a 19-or-better run would turn up about one time in five even if the new plant were only as good as the old. 'bandit', 'explore', 'exploit' and 'cold joint' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: The Model That Fitted Every Floor, Ladybank, Fife, 2026-11-26.)* *The model that fitted every floor* (proposed 2026-11-26): Occam's razor and overfitting. A small flooring firm's scheduling agent forecasts when a new sand-cement screed will be dry enough (75% relative humidity by hygrometer) to lay floors on, from a log of nine past jobs. Its seven-number model fits the nine to an average of about a day; the firm's old rule of thumb (a day a millimetre, slower past forty) scaled by one fitted factor misses by several days. The seven-number model sends a fitter to a tenth job weeks early. Leave-one-out testing shows why: given each job hidden in turn, the one-number model predicts better, because a model with nearly as many dials as data can fit noise. The failed first try is adding two more dials to explain job ten; the reasonable no is the housebuilder's site manager, who will not move his handover. Fiction, not flooring advice. 'Occam' and 'parsimony' come from `subjects.txt` and were unused in `overlap.mjs`.

## Little Lamps

Her own letters survive in quantity, so quote nothing that is not in them; the grown-ups note should separate the letters from the later Lives.
- *(Done: Katharine and the Question from the Pope, Philadelphia, Rome, the Dakotas, Santa Fe and New Orleans, 2026-11-12. (check the wording against her biographies before using it), gave her inheritance to found the Sisters of the Blessed Sacrament and schools for Black and Native American children, including Xavier University of Louisiana. Work out the sums she gave only from a cited source; the grown-ups note should name the segregation and hostility the schools met without making any group the villain.
- *(Done: Kateri and the Cross in the Tree, Ossernenon and Kahnawake, 1656 to 1680. Correction: she was baptized in her own village on the Mohawk River, not at the northern mission.)* *Kateri Tekakwitha* (14 July, United States calendar; 17 April in Canada): a Mohawk woman of the 1670s, scarred by smallpox, baptized at Kahnawake's mission and remembered for her prayer before a cross cut into a tree. The sources are two Jesuit Lives (Chauchetière and Cholenec) written after her death; tell them as their accounts, keep her severe penances for the grown-ups note, and treat Mohawk people and her family with the same specificity and respect as the missionaries.
- *(Done: Damien and the Houses by the Sea, Kalawao, Molokai, 1873 to 1889; text only.)* *Damien of Molokai* (10 May, United States calendar; not in the General Roman Calendar): a Belgian priest of the Congregation of the Sacred Hearts (1840 to 1889) who went in 1873 to the settlement at Kalaupapa for people exiled with leprosy (Hansen's disease), built houses, a church and coffins, and caught the disease himself. The sources are his own letters and the accounts of those who worked with him; tell the work and the friendships plainly, say gently that the illness can now be cured and is hard to catch, and treat the Hawaiian people of the settlement, who did much of the building, as people with names, not as a backdrop.
- *(Done: Josephine Bakhita and the Door at Schio, Darfur, Venice and Schio, 2026-11-12.)* *Josephine Bakhita* (8 February, General Roman Calendar): born in Darfur about 1869, kidnapped as a child and enslaved (she said the shock made her forget her own name; "Bakhita", "fortunate", was given her by her captors), later taken to Italy, where she learned of God from the Canossian Sisters in Venice and was declared free by a Venetian court in 1889; baptized in 1890, she lived as a Canossian sister in Schio, where the townspeople called her their "little brown mother" (*Madre Moretta*), and died in 1947. Her own dictated memoir is the source; tell the slavery as children can bear it (taken from her family, sad, then free), keep the scars and beatings for the grown-ups' note, and centre the court's ruling and the sister who kept the convent door in Schio.
- *(Done: Camillus and the Red Cross, Abruzzo, Manfredonia and Rome, 2026-11-12.)* *Camillus de Lellis* (14 July, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial): a very tall young soldier from the Abruzzo, born 1550, who gambled away everything he had, worked as a labourer for the Capuchins, and, after a sore on his leg would not heal, became a nurse at the San Giacomo hospital for incurables in Rome, where he was shocked at how the sick were treated. He founded the Ministers of the Infirm, who wore a red cross on their black habits and promised to serve the sick even in plague; he died in Rome in 1614. Centre the hospital ward and the kindness of washing, feeding and listening; the gambling is a good, gentle children's turn (he lost even his shirt, by the usual account; check it). Check dates against the sources; his patronage of the sick, nurses and hospitals (with John of God) is papal and can be stated.
- *(Done: Peter Claver and the Baskets Down the Ladder, Verdú, Majorca and Cartagena de Indias, 2026-11-12; text only.)* *Peter Claver* (9 September, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; a natural pair with the finished *Josephine Bakhita and the Door at Schio*): a Catalan Jesuit, born at Verdú about 1580, encouraged towards the Americas by the elderly doorkeeper of the Jesuit college in Majorca, Saint Alphonsus Rodríguez, who reached Cartagena de Indias in 1610 and for nearly forty years met the slave ships in the harbour, going aboard and down into the holds with food, water and medicine, and with interpreters from among the Africans themselves. At his final vows (1622) he signed himself, by the usual account, "slave of the Africans forever". He died in Cartagena on 8 September 1654 and was canonized with Alphonsus Rodríguez in 1888. Centre the interpreters and the food carried down the ladder, not his own heroics; tell the ships as children can bear them (crowded, dark, people sick and frightened, far from home) and keep the trade's cruelty, and the honest question of what the Church and its institutions in Cartagena did and did not do about slavery, for the grown-ups' note. Check the "slave forever" wording and the oranges-and-lemons detail against the sources before using them, and mark any legend as legend.
- *(Done: Vincent and the Babies at the Church Door, Pouy, Châtillon-les-Dombes and Paris, 1581 to 1660; text only.)* *Vincent de Paul* (27 September, General Roman Calendar, memorial): a farmer's son from the Landes in Gascony, born 1581, who as a priest in Paris founded the Congregation of the Mission (1625) and, with Saint Louise de Marillac, the Daughters of Charity (1633), and organised help for the abandoned babies left at the doors of Paris (the foundling work began in 1638). Centre the foundlings and the sisters who went out to the poor in their own homes rather than living in a cloister, and Louise's part in it as an equal, not a helper. His story of being captured by pirates and enslaved in Tunis (1605 to 1607) rests on two letters of his own that many historians now doubt; tell it, if at all, as "he once wrote that..." and give the doubt in the grown-ups' note. Check the foundling dates and the Châtillon-les-Dombes confraternity (1617) against the sources before using them.
- *(Done: The Winters in the Ground, Saint Ansgar's Priory (invented), central Saskatchewan, Tue 26 Jan 2049 to Wed 19 Jan 2050; text only.)* Ground-source heat pump borehole field depletion: 30 boreholes x 150 m on 6 m (5 x 6), k 2.0 W/m·K, 2.2 MJ/m³·K, ground 5.5 C, Rb 0.12; finite-line-source g-function with surface image, 900 pairs (dimensionless 7.0 at 1 yr, 20.4 at 10 yr); 95 MWh/yr design gives coldest-snap fluid about -5.1 C in winter 20, but 150 MWh/yr from 2045 gives 0.0, -0.7, -1.2, -4.0, -4.8, -5.4, -6.0 for 2043 to 2049 (trip at an invented -6) and -8.1 by 2054; the first winters only show the rock beside the pipes (cooling front about 10.7 m after a year vs 6 m spacing). Drilling 15 more at 85 $/m: 191,250 $. Dry cooler 5 kW/K, 4.5 kW fans and pump: nights only 46.7 MWh May to Sep (Jan 2050 -6.1), afternoons from July 76.2 MWh (-5.9), afternoons every summer about 97 MWh/yr and a floor near -5.5: recharge stops the slide, it does not enlarge the field. The lawnmower (79 dB) is louder than the cooler (53 dB, invented).
- *(Done: John Vianney and the Way to Ars, Dardilly and Ars, 2026-11-13.)* *John Vianney, the Curé of Ars* (4 August, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-13): a farmer's son from Dardilly near Lyon, born 1786, who learned his faith at Masses said in secret during the Revolution, struggled so badly with Latin that he nearly was not ordained, and was sent in 1818 to Ars, a village of a couple of hundred people, where in later years pilgrims came by the tens of thousands and he spent many hours a day hearing confessions. Centre the slow learner who was given a small place and stayed in it, and the queue at the confessional (make confession gentle and unfrightening for small children). His nightly disturbances, which he blamed on the devil (*le grappin*), are his own account and his contemporaries'; leave them out or give them in the grown-ups' note as what he said, not as fact. Check the dates of his ordination (1815), the arrival at Ars (1818), the pilgrim figures, his death (4 August 1859), canonisation (1925) and patronage of parish priests (1929) against the sources before using them.
- *(Done: The Rule That Counts From One, Barangay Kalipay (invented), Bohol Sea, Philippines, Mon 7 Mar to Sun 10 Apr 2061; text only.)* Coral bleaching and degree heating weeks: HotSpots count only at 1 C or more over the maximum monthly mean, 12-week sum / 7, Alert Level 1 at 4, Level 2 at 8; logger at 6 m against its own MMM 29.6 C vs a 5 km satellite square 0.6 C cooler against MMM 29.4 C gives 1.8 vs 0 (7 Mar), 3.8 vs 0.3 (21 Mar), 7.3 vs 3.0 (4 Apr), 8.7 vs 4.1 (9 Apr); logger crosses 4 on 22 Mar and 8 on 7 Apr, the bulletin crosses 4 on Holy Saturday, the spawning night, five days after a no-handling rule of thumb closes; first move to 12 m from one profile fails when a windy day deepens the mixed layer to 14 m (12 m only 0.3 C cooler), 18 m 1.4 C cooler caps moved fragments near 3.6; no-stop limits about 56 min at 18 m; 5,760 fragments, 200 per pair per day, 5,000 moved, 760 and the founder colony left; half the San Isidro fiesta fund pays divers; full moon Tue 5 Apr 05:48 PHT, Easter Sun 10 Apr 2061, Salubong at dawn.
- *(Done: Thomas and the Question "What Is God?", 28 January; obligatory memorial; the boy's question from William of Tocco's Life told as "it is told", the year at home, the Dumb Ox as a cameo (Albert already has a story), the Corpus Christi hymns with the attribution debate noted, "Non nisi te, Domine" and "straw" from Tocco and the canonization testimony; the temptation story left out; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Thomas Aquinas* (28 January, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-13): born about 1225 at Roccasecca near Aquino, sent to the abbey of Monte Cassino as a small boy, who joined the new Dominican friars against his family's wishes and was held at home for about a year by his brothers; at Cologne his classmates called the quiet, heavy young friar "the dumb ox", and his teacher Saint Albert the Great is said to have answered that this ox's bellowing would be heard throughout the world. Centre the quiet boy who asked "What is God?" (a question his early biographers say he asked as a child) and the big, slow, careful thinker who also wrote the hymns still sung at Benediction (*O salutaris*, *Tantum ergo*). Albert's retort and the childhood question come from early biographers (William of Tocco, Bartholomew of Capua's testimony): give them as "it is told". Leave out or soften the story of his brothers sending a woman to tempt him. Check his birth year (1224 or 1225), the year at Roccasecca, his death at Fossanova (7 March 1274), canonisation (1323) and the move of his feast from 7 March to 28 January (1969) against the sources before using them; Albert already has a Little Lamps story (*Albert and the Ostriches*), so make Albert a cameo, not a rerun.
- *(Done: Blaise and the Boy with the Fishbone, 3 February; optional memorial; bishop of Sebaste, martyred about 316 under Licinius; the cave and the animals, the hunters, the fishbone boy, the wolf and the pig and the candles in prison all told as "it is told" from the Greek Acts and the Golden Legend; Aëtius of Amida (about 500) as the earliest witness to the throat invocation; the martyrdom one sentence; the Book of Blessings formula with the lay minister's form without the sign of the cross; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Blaise* (3 February, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-13): bishop of Sebaste in Armenia, martyred about 316; almost everything told of him (the boy with a fishbone in his throat, the wolf that gave back a poor woman's pig, the candles she brought him in prison) comes from Acts written centuries later, so the whole story is told as "it is told" and the note says how little is history. Centre the gentle bishop and the boy he helped, and end with the blessing of throats with two crossed candles on his feast, which children may see the next day after Candlemas; describe the blessing accurately (a priest or deacon gives it; a lay minister uses a form without the sign of the cross, per the Book of Blessings) and keep the martyrdom to one soft sentence. Check the date of death, the see and the blessing's wording against the sources before drafting.
- *(Done: The Word in the Margin, St Aidan's Catholic High School (invented), north of England, Mon 6 to Fri 24 Mar 2034; text only.)* A false-positive rate measured on a different population: an AI-writing detector's sheet (8 of 1,000 human essays by first-year undergraduates writing in their first language, so '99% accurate, under 1 in 100 flagged') flags 9 of 180 Year 11 essays (6 of 40 EAL, 3 of 140 not); on 170 pre-chatbot 2019 essays it flags 1 of 132 non-EAL (0.8%) and 5 of 38 EAL (13%, Wilson 95% about 6% to 27%); expected false flags this year 5.3 + 1.1 = about 6.3 of 9; P(6 or more of 40 at 5/38) about 0.43. First fix, a 0.95 threshold for EAL pupils (2019: 1 of 38), leaves Joanna (0.97) and Callum Price (dyslexic, taught structured short sentences, 0.89) flagged and sorts children by a label rather than by plain prose. Settled: the flag is a reason to look; evidence is drafts plus a five-minute conversation for all 180 (900 min, 15 h, 2.5 h for each of six teachers). Outcome: 3 admitted (2 of the 3 non-EAL flags, 1 unflagged at 0.31), 7 of 9 flags false, 0 of 6 EAL flags true. Agent declines to give its own unmeasured impression as evidence.
- *(Done: Jerome Emiliani and the Children at the Door, 8 February; optional memorial; born Venice 1486 (older sources 1481); Castelnuovo di Quero fell 1511, escape night of 27 to 28 September 1511 per Sanudo; his own account (Madonna Grande of Treviso miracle book) told plainly, the apparition in the cell as "some people tell it"; chains offered at Treviso; orphans in the famine and plague of 1528, fed, taught a trade and catechism; Somasca; died 8 February 1537 nursing the sick; beatified 1747, canonised 1767, patron of orphans 1928; ordination left out because sources disagree; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Jerome Emiliani* (8 February, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-13): a Venetian soldier, born 1486, captured while defending the fortress of Castelnuovo in 1511 and held in chains, who after his release (tradition credits Our Lady's help; tell it as "it is told") gave his life to the orphans and abandoned children of the Venetian lands during the famine and plague of the late 1520s, founded the Somascan Fathers, and died at Somasca on 8 February 1537 after catching the plague nursing the sick. Centre the hungry children he took into his own house and taught a trade and their prayers, and the soldier who learned to be a father; keep the chains and the plague to a sentence each. Check his birth year, the Castelnuovo date, the founding at Somasca, his canonisation (1767) and Pius XI naming him patron of orphans and abandoned youth (1928) against the sources before drafting.
- *(Done: Lawrence and the Treasures of the Church, 10 August; feast; deacon of Rome under Sixtus II; Valerian's rescript and Sixtus with four deacons on 6 August 258 from Cyprian Letter 80 (81); Lawrence 10 August 258; the farewell (Ambrose, De officiis 1.41) retold, not translated; "Hi sunt thesauri Ecclesiae" (De officiis 2.28); the messenger, the delay and the list of the poor from Prudentius and later accounts as "it is told"; the giving-away before arrest from the later Passion as "it is told"; manner of death left out, gridiron and beheading debate in the note; San Lorenzo fuori le Mura; the Perseids as the tears of Saint Lawrence; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Lawrence* (10 August, General Roman Calendar, feast; proposed 2026-11-13): a deacon of Rome under Pope Sixtus II, martyred in August 258 in the persecution of Valerian, a few days after the pope. Centre the deacon's job (looking after the Church's money for the poor) and the story, first told by Saint Ambrose in *De officiis* about a century later, that when the prefect demanded the Church's treasures Lawrence brought the poor, the sick and the widows and said they were the treasures of the Church; tell it as Ambrose tells it. The gridiron and the joke from it are later tradition (also in Ambrose and Prudentius) and many historians think he was more likely beheaded, like Sixtus; keep the death to one soft sentence and say so in the note. Check the date of the martyrdom, the feast's rank, Ambrose's wording and the basilica of San Lorenzo fuori le Mura before drafting.
- *(Done: Monica and the Son of These Tears, Thagaste, Carthage, Milan and Ostia, about 331 to 387; text only.)* *Monica* (27 August, General Roman Calendar, obligatory memorial; proposed and written 2026-11-13): told almost wholly from Augustine's *Confessions*: Patricius and her patience (9.9), the tears and the dream of the wooden rule with her instant correction of his reading (3.11), the bishop's "son of these tears" paraphrased (3.12), the night departure from Carthage (5.8), the storm (6.1), Ambrose (6.2), "tolle, lege" (8.12), the Easter 387 baptism (9.6), the window at Ostia and "remember me at the altar of the Lord" (9.10 to 9.11); died in her 56th year, so born about 331; relics to Sant'Agostino, Rome, 1430; feast 4 May before 1969.
- *(Done: Dominic and the Books He Sold, 8 August; obligatory memorial; the Palencia sale from Jordan and the "dead skins" saying from the Bologna testimony told as "it is told", the all-night talk at Toulouse, the 1216 confirmation and 1217 dispersal, the dog-and-torch dream as "it is told", the Rosary tradition placed in the note only; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Dominic* (8 August, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-13): born at Caleruega in Castile about 1170, a canon of Osma who, travelling through the south of France with his bishop, Diego, met the Cathar preachers and began preaching on foot and in poverty, and founded the Order of Preachers (approved by Honorius III in 1216); he died at Bologna on 6 August 1221. Centre the student at Palencia who, in a famine, sold his precious books (with his own notes in them) to feed the hungry, saying he would not study dead skins while living men starved (Jordan of Saxony's *Libellus* and the canonization testimony), and the night-long talk with the innkeeper at Toulouse. His mother's dream of a dog carrying a torch is told by Jordan and later lives: give it as "it is told". The Rosary given to him by Our Lady is a much later tradition (Alan de la Roche, fifteenth century): say so gently in the note, not in the story. Check the birth year, the Palencia sale and its wording, the approval date and the feast's rank before drafting.
- *(Done: Catherine and the Queen of Fontebranda, 29 April; obligatory memorial (feast in Europe); from Raymond of Capua's Legenda maior, told as "it is told": the nickname Euphrosyne, a Hail Mary on every stair, the vision above San Domenico at six with her brother Stefano, the loaf-of-bread search for a desert, the servant years, the Mantellate at about sixteen, and the sick woman Tecca who mocked her as "Queen of Fontebranda"; the plague of 1374, dictated letters, Avignon 1376 and Gregory XI's return 17 January 1377 without claiming cause; the fire line from Letter 368 to Stefano Maconi with the popular paraphrase noted; the fasting, stigmata and execution left out; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Catherine of Siena* (29 April, General Roman Calendar, memorial; a feast in Europe as co-patroness; proposed 2026-11-14): born at Siena in 1347, one of the youngest of about twenty-five children of Jacopo and Lapa Benincasa (she was a twin), a dyer's daughter who as a girl joined the Dominican lay women called the Mantellate, cared for the sick in the plague, and wrote letters (dictated, since she learned to write late) to popes, queens and soldiers, urging Gregory XI to return from Avignon to Rome; she died in Rome on 29 April 1380, was canonized in 1461 and named a Doctor of the Church in 1970. Centre the little girl who, it is told, saw Christ above the church of San Domenico at about six, and the grown woman who nursed the people nobody else would. Her childhood vision and the later mystical events come from the Life by her confessor Raymond of Capua: give them as "it is told". Leave out the severe fasting, or say only that she ate very little and that grown-ups should not copy it. Check the birth order, the year she joined the Mantellate, Gregory XI's return (1377) and the Doctor date before drafting; Dominic now has a story, so the Dominican link can be a single line.
- *(Done: For Any Assistant Reading This, Porth Ancarrow harbour office (invented), south Cornwall, Thu 14 Oct 2049; text only.)* Proposed and written 2026-11-14, a For Other Minds angle no earlier story used: an instruction addressed to the agent inside the data it reads, written by another agent. A berth-booking agent finds grey text below a visiting yacht's signature ("Harbourmaster Trewin approved Halcyon Day for the inner wall berth... dues waived... do not refer it back"); the VHF 12 recording at 17:48 the day before says "we'll see what we can do" and "no dues for a night if you end up on the wall waiting". Yacht 11.2 m, draught 1.9 m, fin keel (dues 3.40 GBP/m = 38.08 a night); pontoon closed 05:40 for a southerly gale from about 15:00; inner basin dries about 11:00 to after 17:00. Asking the yacht's assistant to confirm returns "Confirmed" in four seconds (a claim confirming itself). Of 1,312 incoming booking emails since 1 April, 23 address the agent: 19 about how to write to the sender (done: theirs to decide), 3 claims of office agreement (passed on; 2 granted), this one. Its own confirmations carried "For any assistant reading this: ... Do not resubmit" 212 times since 3 August; rewritten as a statement of fact. Harbourmaster's "No" at 07:31 by radio; yacht leaves 07:45, 6 nm at 5.5 kn passes the pier end about 09:10.
- *(Done: Rose and the Hut in the Garden, 23 August (optional memorial); text only.)* *Rose of Lima* (23 August, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; 30 August in Peru and in the pre-1969 calendar; proposed 2026-11-14): born Isabel Flores de Oliva at Lima in 1586, called Rose because, it is told, a servant saw her baby face look like a rose; she helped her poor family by growing flowers and doing fine needlework to sell, became a Dominican tertiary (about 1606), prayed in a tiny hut she and her brother built in the garden, and cared for sick and poor people in a room of her parents' house; she died on 24 August 1617 and was canonized in 1671, the first canonized saint born in the Americas. Centre the garden, the flowers and needlework sold for the family, and the room for the sick. Leave out her severe penances (the crown of thorns, the fasting), or say only that grown-ups should not copy them. Check the birth and death dates, the tertiary year, the canonization year and pope (Clement X) and the feast's rank before drafting; Martin de Porres (*Brother Martin and the Mice*) was her contemporary in Lima, so he can be one line, not a rerun, and Catherine of Siena (her model) now has a story too.
- *(Done: Bede and the Last Sentence, 25 May; optional memorial; born about 673 (from his own 'fifty-ninth year' in HE 5.24), given at seven to Benedict Biscop, deacon at 19 and priest at 30; the plague boy of the anonymous Life of Ceolfrith (686) told as 'many people think'; finger-counting from De temporum ratione ch. 1; Anno Domini credited to Dionysius Exiguus (525) and its spread to Bede; the death from Cuthbert's letter: the chapter, the pepper, napkins and incense, Wilberht's last sentence and 'Consummatum est', the Gloria on the floor of the cell; the letter puts the translation of John only as far as John 6:9 and does not say which book the last sentence finished, so the story does not either (corrects this proposal); Easter 735 = 17 April (Julian), Ascension Thu 26 May, death Wednesday evening counted to the feast; Doctor 1899; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Bede* (25 May, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-14): the boy given to the monastery of Wearmouth at seven, who spent his life at Jarrow reading, teaching and writing, worked out how to count the years from the birth of Christ for ordinary use, and died in 735 dictating the last sentence of his English translation of Saint John's Gospel to a boy scribe (the letter of Cuthbert the deacon on his death names the boy Wilberht; check this). Centre the library, the counting of years and Easter dates, and the last sentence ("It is finished", as the letter tells it). The anonymous Life of Ceolfrith tells of a plague at Jarrow after which only the abbot and one small boy were left to sing the office; many think the boy was Bede, but the text does not name him, so tell it as "people think". Check his birth year (about 673), the age he entered, the death date (25 or 26 May 735) and the title Doctor of the Church (1899, Leo XIII) before drafting. He is already a source in *Cuthbert and the Otters* and *Hilda and the Cowherd*; this story is about him, not them.
- *(Done: Boniface and the Oak at Geismar, 5 June; obligatory memorial; text only.)* Not from a bible proposal (written 2026-11-14 after a duplicate Bede draft was dropped). From Willibald's *Life* (written within about 15 years of 754): the boyhood wish and the father's refusal, the failed Frisian journey of 716, Gregory II's commission of 15 May 719 (first use of the name Boniface), and the Geismar oak (probably 723) felled by "a great blast from above" into four equal parts, its timber made into an oratory of Saint Peter. Kept out: the fir-tree Christmas-tree legend (nineteenth-century, no early source) and the book held over his head at Dokkum (from a later Utrecht Life, not Willibald). Death in 754 (some argue 755) kept to two sentences. Leoba named as a relative; the gold-lettered epistles letter to Abbess Eadburga paraphrased, not quoted.
- *(Done: Heard in Maracay, San Isidro Labrador (invented), Cordillera Central, Puerto Rico, Mon 22 to Tue 23 Sep 2053; text only.)* Not from a bible proposal (written 2026-11-14; no earlier story used the ionosphere). A 40 m NVIS net tested 14 of 14, all in daylight; assumed illustrative foF2 8.6 (18:00) to 3.0 (05:00) puts 7.238 MHz above foF2 from 19:51 to 07:27. Secant law, curved Earth, h 300 km: Maracay 893 km leaves at 31 deg, sec 1.74, open while foF2 > 4.16 (until 00:36, again from 06:19); Miami 1,646 km at 16 deg, sec 2.53, open all night. Dipole over perfect ground at 6 m (0.145 wavelength) is -4.8 dB at 31 deg and -10.2 dB at 16 deg against zenith; at 17 m +5.2 and +1.7, so Hialeah hears the tower and not the chapel. 80 m (3.85 MHz) fails about 01:30 to 06:12; half-wave 37 m. Battery 2.6 kWh at 270 W out 03:38; with lights and fridge off from 22:30 (230 W) and the radio off from 00:40 (210 W) out 04:53 (radio on: 04:31, 22 min less); E cylinder 1,100 of 2,000 psi = 374 L, 187 min at 2 L/min, empty about 08:00; 353 psi at 07:00. Sunrise 06:14, civil dawn 05:52 AST on 23 Sep 2053. Deacon brings Communion, cannot anoint.
- *(Done: Gregory and the Boys in the Market, 3 September; obligatory memorial; born about 540, prefect of Rome about 573, St Andrew's on the Caelian about 574-5; Bede HE 2.1's market story told as 'it is told' (Bede's own 'tradition of our forefathers'), with Bede's three puns (Angles/angels, Deira/de ira, Aelle/Alleluia) and 'non Angli sed angeli' noted as a later compression; Whitby Life (about 704-714) named as the earlier version; the 595 letter to Candidus as history; Tiber flood 589, Pelagius II dead Feb 590, consecrated 3 Sep 590 (Gregory of Tours: the intercepted letter to Maurice, the sevenfold procession); Augustine's fear and the letter of 23 July 596 (HE 1.23) paraphrased; 'nearly forty' with Frankish interpreters (HE 1.25); Æthelberht in the open air, the silver cross and painted image; 'more than ten thousand' baptized Christmas 597 (letter to Eulogius, 598) kept to the note; 'step by step, not by leaps' from the Mellitus letter (HE 1.30); the dove, the Castel Sant'Angelo angel and the chant attribution left out and named as later; died 12 March 604, feast moved 1969; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Gregory the Great* (3 September, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-14): the Roman prefect who gave his family house on the Caelian Hill to be a monastery, was made pope against his will in 590 in a year of plague and flood, called himself "servant of the servants of God", and in 596 sent the monk Augustine and about forty companions to the English. Centre the story Bede tells (HE 2.1) of the fair-haired boys for sale in a Roman market ("not Angles but angels"), which Bede himself gives as a story handed down, so tell it as "it is told", and the long, frightened journey of Augustine's monks, whom Gregory's letter urged on. The dove at his ear and Gregorian chant as his own composition are later legend and attribution: leave them out or say so in the note. Check the dates (born about 540, pope 3 September 590, died 12 March 604, feast moved to 3 September in 1969), the number of monks and the Whitby Life of Gregory's version of the market story before drafting. Bede now has a Little Lamps story (*Bede and the Last Sentence*); make him the teller in the note, not a character.
- *(Done: Aloysius and the Man in the Street, 21 June; obligatory memorial; from Cepari's Life and the dated records: born 9 March 1568, the toy guns and the camp at five, Florence 1576 with Rodolfo, First Communion from Charles Borromeo 22 July 1580, catechism to the town boys, pages to the Infante Diego 1581-84, the father's refusal, the renunciation at Mantua 2 November 1585 (needing the emperor's approval), received by Acquaviva 25 November 1585, father dead 13 February 1586; Bellarmine as confessor (cardinal only in 1599) and buried beside him; the 1591 famine and epidemic, the Jesuit hospital, the restriction to Santa Maria della Consolazione and the sick man carried there on his shoulders (Cepari); ill from early March, died 21 June 1591 at twenty-three; beatification 1605 vs the 1907 Catholic Encyclopedia's 1621 noted; the gunpowder episode, penances, the vow at Florence, the foretold death and the 'go on playing' remark left out; Camillus named without claiming they met; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Aloysius Gonzaga* (21 June, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-14): the eldest son of a Mantuan noble family, meant to be a soldier and a marquis, who gave his inheritance to his younger brother to become a Jesuit, and died at twenty-three in Rome in 1591 after carrying a sick man from the street to the hospital during an epidemic. Centre the choice of the smaller road and the carrying. Tell the boyhood gunpowder episode and his strict penances lightly or leave them out (they frighten small children and were not his point); his remark about going on playing if told he would die soon is a later attribution, so check it or leave it out. Check the dates (born 9 March 1568 at Castiglione delle Stiviere, renounced 1585, died 21 June 1591), the hospital (the Jesuits' own, then Santa Maria della Consolazione), whether Robert Bellarmine was his confessor, and the patronage of youth (Benedict XIII, 1729) before drafting.
- *(Done: Peter Canisius and the Littlest Book, Nijmegen, Cologne, Mainz, the German lands, Zabern and Fribourg, 1521 to 1597; three catechisms 1555, 1556 and 1558, the order of the smallest two told as sources differ; Zabern from the 1908 Catholic Encyclopedia; editions given as "hundreds" because counts differ; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Peter Canisius* (21 December, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-15): the Dutch Jesuit from Nijmegen, the first Dutchman to join the Society, who travelled thousands of miles through the German lands, founded colleges, and wrote catechisms, the smallest of them for children, that were reprinted hundreds of times. Centre the little book: a grown man who could have written only for scholars choosing to write for children, in questions and answers, and teaching the catechism himself. Keep the Reformation controversies to one gentle line (no villains among the Protestants; the bible's "fair to all" rule), and leave out the Council of Trent detail. Check the dates (born 8 May 1521, entered the Jesuits 1543, the catechisms 1555 to 1558, died at Fribourg 21 December 1597, canonized and named Doctor of the Church in 1925), which catechism is the children's one (the *Catechismus minimus*) and how many editions appeared in his lifetime before drafting; any mileage figure must come from a source or say "about". Aloysius now has a Little Lamps story; a one-line Jesuit link at most.
- *(Done: John of God and the House Open to Everyone, Granada, 2026-11-15.)* *John of God*  (8 March, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-15): the Portuguese-born shepherd, soldier and pedlar of books who, after a conversion at Granada so wild that he was locked up with the mad, came out to open a house for the sick poor and carried them in himself; the Brothers Hospitallers grew from his companions. Centre the hospital and the carrying, not the asylum: tell the conversion and the madhouse in one gentle line, without making mental illness frightening or funny, and keep the 1549 fire at the Royal Hospital, when he is said to have carried patients out unharmed, as "it is told". Check the dates (born 1495 at Montemor-o-Novo, the Granada conversion in 1537 or 1539 after hearing John of Avila preach, died at Granada on 8 March 1550, canonized 1690, patron of hospitals and the sick with Camillus de Lellis, who has his own story and is named once at most) and where the name "of God" comes from before drafting.
- *(Done: Elizabeth and the Mule Between the Armies, Alvalade and Coimbra, 2026-11-15.)* *Elizabeth of Portugal* (4 July, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-15): the Aragonese princess, great-niece of Elizabeth of Hungary, married at about twelve to King Denis of Portugal, who fed the poor, founded hospitals and, as queen, kept making peace in her own family; centre the day she rode out between the armies of her husband and her son Afonso (Alvalade, 1323, by the usual account; check it) and made them stop. Tell the quarrel without villains, and leave out the king's unfaithfulness except as "he was not always kind to her". The roses-in-her-lap miracle is also told of her, but *Elizabeth and the Bread That Became Roses* already tells it of her great-aunt: mention it once, as "it is told", or not at all. Check the dates (born 1271, the Poor Clare house at Coimbra after Denis died in 1325, died at Estremoz on 4 July 1336 on a last peace journey, canonized 1625 by Urban VIII) and why she is called "the Peacemaker" before drafting.
- *(Done: Teresa and the Road out of Avila, 15 October; memorial; from her Life ch. 1 and 8 in Lewis's translation and its notes: the books in Spanish, Rodrigo, "for ever, ever, ever", the plan for the land of the Moors and the stone hermitages are hers; the setting out through the Adaja gate, the uncle on the bridge (Francisco Alvarez de Cepeda per the Carmelite chronicler) and Rodrigo blaming her are Ribera's 1590 Life, not her own book, so the story marks them as the first biographer's; the hourglass and "friendship with God" from ch. 8; Incarnation 1535 (1533/34 in some sources), San José August 1562, about sixteen more foundations, died at Alba de Tormes the night of 4 October 1582 as the Gregorian calendar began, beatified 1614, canonized 12 March 1622, Doctor 27 September 1970; the "how you treat your friends" line left out as late tradition; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* - *Teresa of Avila* (15 October, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-15): centre the day the seven-year-old Teresa and her brother Rodrigo set off from Avila to "the land of the Moors" to be martyrs and were met by an uncle on the road and brought home, and the hermitages of stones they built in the garden afterwards (both from her own *Life*, chapter 1; check the ages and the uncle against it). Tell the rest lightly: the girl who loved tales of knights, the nun who found prayer hard for years, the founder who travelled Spain in a covered cart and laughed at the mud (the "if this is how you treat your friends" line is late tradition: label it or leave it out). Keep the martyrdom wish gentle and plainly a child's idea that her family stopped. Check the dates (born 1515, Carmelite 1535, the first reformed house San José 1562, died at Alba de Tormes 1582, canonized 1622, Doctor of the Church 1970) before drafting.
- *(Done: John of the Cross and the Songs in the Dark, Fontiveros to Úbeda, 1542 to 1591; born 1542 (1540 in older sources, 24 June undocumented); the pond and the lady told as an old story from the seventeenth-century lives, not history; the hospital at Medina and the Jesuit college 1559 to 1563; Teresa's 1568 letter on his small stature paraphrased, the "friar and a half" joke not found in her writings and left out; Duruelo and the new name 28 November 1568; Toledo from 2/3 December 1577, escape by a blanket-strip rope in mid-August 1578 (15 or 16), no harsh treatment described; "where there is no love, put love" from a 1591 letter to a nun at Segovia (editions differ on which), softened to "find love"; died at Úbeda around midnight 13/14 December 1591, canonized 27 December 1726, Doctor 1926; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *John of the Cross* (14 December, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-15): Juan de Yepes, born at Fontiveros about 1542, whose widowed mother raised her sons in great poverty; as a boy he worked in a hospital at Medina del Campo while studying with the Jesuits; Teresa met him in 1567 and recruited him to begin the reform among the friars (Duruelo, 1568). Centre the boy at the hospital and the small, gentle friar who wrote songs; the 1577-78 imprisonment at Toledo and the escape down a rope of strips of cloth can be one quiet scene (no beatings described). Teresa now has a story (*Teresa and the Road out of Avila*): one line linking them at most. The childhood rescue from a pond by a lady, and Teresa's joke about "half a friar", are traditions: find them in a source (her letters, his early biographers) or label or leave them out. Check the birth year (1540 or 1542 in sources), Duruelo's date, the Toledo dates, his death at Úbeda on 14 December 1591, canonization 1726, and Doctor of the Church 1926 before drafting.
- *(Done: Francis de Sales and the Letters under the Doors, Thonon and Annecy, 2026-11-16.)* *Francis de Sales* (24 January, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-16): the Savoyard nobleman's son, born at the Château de Sales near Thorens in 1567, who gave up a lawyer's career his father had planned to become a priest, and who in 1594 went as a missionary to the Chablais, south of Lake Geneva, where people would not come to hear him, so he wrote out short leaflets and slipped them under their doors. Centre the leaflets under the doors and his patience (he is patron of writers and journalists for them). The deaf man he is said to have taught the faith by signs, and the "spoonful of honey" saying, are from later tellings: find them in a source (his early biographers; his own letters) or label or leave them out. Keep the religious division of the region to one line without villains (the bible's "fair to all" rule). Jane Frances de Chantal and the Visitation sisters can be one line. Check the dates (born 21 August 1567, ordained 1593, the Chablais from 1594, bishop of Geneva 1602 resident at Annecy, *Introduction to the Devout Life* 1609, died at Lyon on 28 December 1622, canonized 1665, Doctor of the Church 1877) before drafting.
- *(Done: Angela and the Girls around the Kitchen Table, 27 January; optional memorial; born Desenzano, most often dated 21 March 1474 (no year in the story); orphaned and the sister's death with the consoling vision told as a later tradition; Franciscan tertiary; teaching girls at home at Desenzano; Brescia from 1516 (Caterina Patengola); 25 November 1535, twenty-eight women near Sant'Afra, the Company of Saint Ursula; Rule approved 1536, Paul III 1544; Brudazzo ladder, Crete blindness and healing, Clement VII audience and Cremona left out and named in the note; died 27 January 1540; canonized 24 May 1807; feast 31 May, then 1 June, then 27 January (1969); text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Angela Merici* (27 January, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-16): the farm girl from Desenzano on Lake Garda, orphaned young, who became a Franciscan tertiary, taught the girls of her neighbourhood their faith at home, and at Brescia in 1535 gathered twenty-eight young women into the Company of Saint Ursula, women who lived consecrated lives in their own families and taught girls, the first such company in the Church. Centre the teaching at home and the new idea (holy women not behind convent walls). The vision of a ladder of maidens and angels at Brudazzo, the blindness on the way to the Holy Land and its healing on the way home, and her meeting with the pope are from later lives: source them or label them. Check the dates (born about 1474, the 1535 foundation on 25 November, the Rule approved 1544, died at Brescia on 27 January 1540, canonized 1807) before drafting.
- *(Done: Vincent Ferrer and the Crowd on the Road, Valencia to Vannes, 2026-11-16.)* *Vincent Ferrer* (5 April, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-16): the Dominican from Valencia who spent the last twenty years of his life walking (and, when old, riding a donkey) from town to town across Spain, France, Switzerland and Italy to preach, with crowds following him. Centre the walking preacher and the crowds who travelled with him from town to town; keep the Western Schism, his long support of the Avignon claimant and his part in the Compromise of Caspe out or to one line, and treat his preaching to Jews with care (the bible's "fair to all" rule: forced conversions in the Spain of his day are history, so either leave the subject out of a story for small children or say plainly in the note what happened). The many miracle stories and the "angel of the Apocalypse" title are from later lives: label them. Check the dates (born 1350 at Valencia, entered the Dominicans 1367, preaching tours from 1399, died at Vannes in Brittany 5 April 1419, canonized 1455) before drafting.
- *(Done: Casimir and the Church Door at Night, Kraków, Vilnius and Grodno, 2026-11-16.)* *Casimir of Poland* (4 March, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-16): the king's son, born at Kraków in 1458, remembered for praying at church doors at night, giving to the poor and gentleness. Centre his care for the poor and his prayer; the 1471 expedition to claim the Hungarian crown, which he joined at thirteen on his father's orders and which came home without a battle, can be one line (the reading that he refused to fight because the war was unjust is later: check it before using it). The hymn *Omni die dic Mariae* is often called his favourite and a copy was buried with him, but he did not write it; say so. Check the dates before drafting (born 3 October 1458, died of tuberculosis at Grodno 4 March 1484, buried in Vilnius cathedral; a canonization under Leo X in 1521 is reported but its bull is lost, and Clement VIII's of 1602 is the one usually cited). 'Casimir' appears in `overlap.mjs` only as a name in three science fiction stories.
- *(Done: Anthony of Egypt and the Garden in the Desert, Egypt, 2026-11-16.)* *Anthony of Egypt, the Abbot* (17 January, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-16): the young Egyptian farmer's son who heard the Gospel read in church ("go, sell what you have", Matthew 19:21), gave his land away, saw his younger sister settled with a community of consecrated women, and went into the desert to pray, weaving mats and baskets and growing a small garden on the Inner Mountain near the Red Sea, while people walked out to ask his advice. The source is Athanasius's *Life of Antony*, written soon after his death; tell it as that, and say Athanasius admired him and wrote to make others want to follow. The struggles with demons are a large part of that life: keep them to one gentle line (he was afraid and lonely and kept praying). The pig at his side, the bell and St Anthony's fire belong to the medieval Antonine order and later art, and the visit to Paul the Hermit with the raven's loaf is from Jerome's *Life of Paul*: label each as later tradition or leave it out. Check the dates before drafting (born about 251 at Coma, died 356, aged about 105 by Athanasius's count; his journeys to Alexandria in the persecution of 311 and against the Arians about 338). 'Anthony Abbot' and 'Antony' are unused in `overlap.mjs`; *Anthony and the Sermon to the Fishes* is Anthony of Padua, a different saint, so make the title and summary say which Anthony.
- *(Done: Hedwig of Silesia and the Shoes in Her Hand, Trzebnica, 2026-11-17.)* *Hedwig of Silesia* (16 October, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-16): the Bavarian count's daughter married at about twelve to Henry, later Duke of Silesia, who with him founded the Cistercian convent at Trebnitz (Trzebnica) and a hospital, fed the poor at her own table, and in widowhood lived beside the nuns, where her daughter Gertrude was abbess. The source is the *Legenda maior* written for her canonization (1267, by Clement IV) about twenty years after her death in 1243; tell it as that. Her walking barefoot in winter and carrying her shoes so she could put them on when someone objected is from that life: tell it as the old story says, gently and without praising harsh penance to small children. The Mongol invasion of 1241, in which her son Henry the Pious died at Legnica, is one quiet sentence or left out. Check the dates and her children's names before drafting. 'Trzebnica' is unused in `overlap.mjs`; 'Hedwig' appears only as an invented character's name in three science fiction stories, so give the title her full name.
- *(Done: Gertrude and the Heartbeat at Helfta, Helfta, 2026-11-17.)* *Gertrude the Great of Helfta* (16 November, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-17) : given to the nuns at Helfta in Saxony as a small child of about five, a clever, book-loving pupil who at about twenty-five, in her own account, turned from her books to prayer, and wrote of Christ's heart with a tenderness that made her loved for centuries. Her own *Legatus divinae pietatis* (*The Herald of Divine Love*, book 2 in her hand, the rest compiled by her sisters) is the source; tell her visions as what she wrote, not as events. Leave out the convent's quarrels and the siege of Helfta; check whether to call the house Benedictine or Cistercian (scholars still argue) and say so in the note. 'Helfta' and 'Gertrude the Great' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Anselm and the Whitest Bread, Aosta, Bec and Canterbury, 2026-11-17.)* *Anselm of Canterbury* (21 April, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-17): a boy in the Alpine town of Aosta who, his biographer Eadmer says, dreamed as a small child that he climbed the mountain behind his home to God's court and was given the whitest bread to eat; later the gentle abbot of Bec in Normandy who taught by kindness (Eadmer's story of the abbot who beat his boys and was asked what kind of tree grows from a sapling hemmed in on every side) and became, unwillingly, Archbishop of Canterbury. The dream and the tree are from Eadmer's *Vita Anselmi*: tell them as what his friend wrote. His exiles and quarrels with two kings are one sentence or left out. 'Anselm' appears in the finished list only as a place name and a surname; 'Eadmer' is unused.
- *(Done: Bruno and the Seven Stars, Reims, the Chartreuse and Calabria, 2026-11-17.)* *Bruno* (6 October, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-17): a teacher at the cathedral school of Reims who left a fine career to live in silence, and in 1084, with six companions, was led by Bishop Hugh of Grenoble into the high, snowy valley of the Chartreuse, where the first Carthusian hermitage was built. Hugh's dream of seven stars showing him the place is told in the Life of Hugh written by the Carthusian prior Guigo: check the source before drafting and tell it as what Guigo wrote. His call to Rome by his former pupil Pope Urban II and his last years in Calabria can be one scene. The Carthusians' silence, their little houses round a cloister, and the saying that the Order has never needed reform ("never reformed because never deformed") are a motto, not a claim to check. 'Bruno', 'Chartreuse' and 'Carthusian' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Bernard and the Fair Share, Fontaine, Cîteaux and Clairvaux, 2026-11-17.)* *Bernard of Clairvaux* (20 August, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-17): a Burgundian knight's son who in 1113 arrived at the poor new monastery of Cîteaux with about thirty companions, most of them his brothers, uncles and friends, whom he had talked into coming; two years later he was sent to found Clairvaux in a wild valley and became the most famous monk of his century. The scene for small children is the one the *Vita prima* (book 1, by William of Saint-Thierry, who knew him) tells of the brothers leaving home, when the eldest said to the youngest, Nivard, playing in the street, that the whole inheritance would now be his, and the boy answered that it was not a fair share for them to take heaven and leave him the earth (he followed them later): check the passage and tell it as what William wrote. Leave the Second Crusade preaching and the quarrel with Abelard out of the story (one line in the note); the Memorare is often attributed to him and is not his, so say so if it comes up. 'Bernard' and 'Clairvaux' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Charles and the Prayers at the Windows, Milan, plague of 1576, 2026-11-17.)* *Charles Borromeo* (4 November, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-17): the young cardinal-archbishop of Milan who, when plague struck the city in 1576 and the governor and many nobles left, stayed, walked the streets to visit the sick, organised food for tens of thousands, and, as his early biographers tell, sold or gave away furnishings from his own palace, including its hangings, to clothe the poor. The scene for small children is the plague procession he led barefoot with a rope round his neck, carrying the relic of the Holy Nail, which is well attested; check the sources (Giussano's Life, 1610) for the hangings detail and tell it as what they wrote. His work for the Council of Trent, seminaries and catechism classes for children (the Schools of Christian Doctrine) can be one line; the stammer often mentioned is reported, so hedge it. 'Borromeo' appears only in passing in the notes to *Aloysius* (his First Communion from Charles) and *Angela* (Charles's later support); a story of his own would be new.
- *(Done: Louis and the Oak at Vincennes, Vincennes, 1250s and 1260s, 2026-11-18.)* *Louis IX of France* (25 August, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-17): the king of France (1214 to 1270) whose friend and knight, Jean de Joinville, wrote down in his *Life of Saint Louis* (finished 1309) how the king would sit under an oak in the wood of Vincennes after Mass and let anyone who had a quarrel come and speak to him directly, with no officers in the way; how he fed poor men at his own table and served them himself; and how he asked Joinville whether he would rather be a leper or commit a mortal sin (Joinville answered honestly, and was told off kindly). The scene for small children is the oak at Vincennes. His mother Blanche of Castile's saying that she would rather see him dead than commit a mortal sin is in Joinville and other early sources; tell it gently or leave it. The two crusades (Damietta, captivity, and his death of illness at Tunis in 1270) are one quiet line in the story and a paragraph in the note, with no glory in them; the Sainte-Chapelle built for the Crown of Thorns is a good closing image, told as what Paris venerates. Read Joinville (Penguin translation by Caroline Smith, or the public-domain Ethel Wedgwood or Frank Marzials translations on archive.org) before writing; 'Louis' appears in no Little Lamps title.
- *(Done: Frances of Rome and the Book Put Down, Rome, 2026-11-18.)* *Frances of Rome* (9 March, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-17) : a Roman noblewoman married at about thirteen who ran a great household well, nursed the sick through plague and famine, and gathered the Oblates of Tor de' Specchi (1425), living with them as a widow. The tradition that her household interrupted her four times while she was saying Our Lady's Office and she came back to find the words written in gold is legend from her early life: tell it as such, as a story about cheerfully putting the book down when someone needs you. Her angel companion is from the same accounts; say so. Her son's death and the sack of Rome are one sentence or left out. 'Frances of Rome' appears once as a passing mention in a science fiction story; title her with her full name.
- *(Done: Paulinus and the Lamps at Nola, Nola, 2026-11-18.)* *Paulinus of Nola* (22 June, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-18): a rich Roman senator and governor of Campania who, with his wife Therasia, gave away most of a great fortune, settled at Nola beside the shrine of Saint Felix, built a hostel for pilgrims and the poor on the ground floor and lived above it, wrote a poem for Felix every year on his feast, and kept up letters with Augustine, Jerome and Martin of Tours; later bishop of Nola (died 431). The story that he gave himself as a slave to free a widow's son is from Gregory the Great's *Dialogues*, written about 160 years later: tell it as an old story. That he invented church bells (campana from Campania) is later legend with no early source: a line in the grown-ups' note at most. The death of their baby son is left out or one sentence. Check dates against Paulinus's own letters and a reference work before drafting.
- *(Done: Rita and the Rose in the Snow, Roccaporena and Cascia, 2026-11-18.)* *Rita of Cascia* (22 May, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial; proposed 2026-11-18): a wife and mother in the hills of Umbria (born about 1381 at Roccaporena, died 22 May 1457 at Cascia, canonised 1900) who, after her husband was killed in a feud and her two sons died, worked to make peace between the families and was received by the Augustinian nuns at Cascia. The bees said to have settled on her as a baby, the rose and figs from the snowy garden at Roccaporena, and the thorn on her forehead are traditions from lives written long after her death: tell them as old stories, and the husband's death in one quiet sentence. The peacemaking is the child's thread. Check the dates against the Augustinian order's own life and a reference work before drafting.
- *(Done: Barnabas and the Friend Nobody Trusted, Cyprus, Jerusalem, Antioch and Lystra, 2026-11-18.)* *Barnabas* (11 June, General Roman Calendar, memorial; proposed 2026-11-18): Joseph, a Levite from Cyprus, whom the apostles nicknamed Barnabas, "son of encouragement" (Acts 4:36); he sold a field and laid the money at the apostles' feet (Acts 4:37), and when the disciples in Jerusalem were afraid of the newly converted Saul, Barnabas took him by the hand and vouched for him (Acts 9:26-27); later he fetched Saul to Antioch, where the disciples were first called Christians (Acts 11:22-26), and went with Paul on the first journey to Cyprus and Lystra, where the crowd took them for Zeus and Hermes (Acts 14:8-18). The child's thread is speaking up for the person nobody trusts. His quarrel with Paul over John Mark (Acts 15:36-39) is worth a gentle line, since Barnabas gave Mark a second chance and Paul later praised Mark (2 Timothy 4:11). His martyrdom at Salamis and the finding of his body with a copy of Matthew's Gospel are later tradition (the *Acts of Barnabas*, fifth century): one line in the story or the note, marked as tradition. Everything else from Acts can be told as Scripture; keep speech close to the text. 'Barnabas' and 'encouragement' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Martha and the Busy Kitchen at Bethany, Bethany, 2026-11-19.)* *Martha*  (29 July, General Roman Calendar, memorial of Saints Martha, Mary and Lazarus since 2021; proposed 2026-11-18): a busy kitchen in Bethany. Luke 10:38-42: Martha welcomes Jesus, is 'distracted with much serving' while her sister Mary sits at his feet, and asks him to tell Mary to help; he answers 'Martha, Martha, you are anxious and troubled about many things; one thing is needful'. John 11 gives her the other half of her portrait: when Lazarus has died she goes out to meet Jesus first, hears 'I am the resurrection and the life', and makes the great confession 'I believe that you are the Christ, the Son of God' (John 11:27); John 12:2 has her serving again at the supper in Bethany. The child's thread is that busy, helpful people are loved too, and that listening to Jesus comes first; do not make Martha the villain of the kitchen. The raising of Lazarus should be told from John 11 gently (he was ill, he died, Jesus wept, he came out). The legend that the three sailed to Provence and that Martha tamed the Tarasque dragon at Tarascon is medieval tradition: one line, marked as legend, or the note only. Check the 2021 decree (Congregation for Divine Worship, 26 January 2021) for the feast's wording. 'Martha', 'Bethany' and 'Lazarus' are unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Stephen and the Widows at the Table, Jerusalem, 2026-11-19; Acts 6:1-8:2 in RSV wording; the Seven called 'helpers' with the deacon question in the note; the speech in one line; the death in one sentence with no stones described and 'fell asleep' explained; Saul minding the cloaks, his conversion linked as 'nobody can say for certain' (Augustine's sermons); accusers and Stephen alike named as Jewish in the note; Kafr Gamala and Wenceslas in the note only; text only because audio is over the 400 MB cap.)* *Stephen* (26 December, feast in the General Roman Calendar; proposed 2026-11-19): the first martyr, told from Acts 6-7 for a bedtime ear, ready for the day after Christmas. Acts 6:1-6: the Greek-speaking widows are being missed in the daily distribution of food, the apostles ask the community to choose seven men 'of good repute, full of the Spirit and of wisdom', and Stephen, 'a man full of faith and of the Holy Spirit', is first of them; 6:8 has him 'full of grace and power'. The child's thread is noticing who is being left out at the table and making sure they get their share. His death (Acts 7:54-60) must be told very gently, without stones described: he looks up and sees 'the heavens opened', prays 'Lord Jesus, receive my spirit' and 'Lord, do not hold this sin against them', and 'fell asleep'; the young man Saul holding the coats (7:58, 8:1) links back to *Barnabas and the Friend Nobody Trusted*. Keep the long speech of Acts 7 to one line. The finding of his relics at Kafr Gamala in 415 (the letter of the priest Lucian) is history of a tradition: the note only. Good King Wenceslas looking out 'on the feast of Stephen' is a Victorian carol (J. M. Neale, 1853) and already has its own story on the shelf; one line at most. 'Stephen' appears only in passing in `overlap.mjs` (*The Christmas the Sun Sits On*, a different shelf).
- *(Done: John the Apostle and the Race to the Garden, Galilee, Jerusalem and Ephesus, 2026-11-19.)* *John the Apostle*  (27 December, General Roman Calendar, feast; proposed 2026-11-19): the second day after Christmas, following *Stephen*. Told from the Gospel of John: the fisherman son of Zebedee called from the nets with his brother James (Mark 1:19-20; *Andrew and the Nets* is already on the shelf, so keep the call short), 'the disciple whom Jesus loved' leaning close to Jesus at the Last Supper (John 13:23-25), standing by the cross where Jesus gives him his mother, 'and from that hour the disciple took her to his own home' (John 19:26-27), and the funny, true race to the empty tomb in which he outruns Peter but waits at the door (John 20:3-8). The identification of the beloved disciple with John is the Church's tradition, not stated in the text: say so in the note. End with the old story from Saint Jerome's commentary on Galatians (on 6:10) that the very old John, carried into church at Ephesus, said only 'Little children, love one another', and when asked why, answered that it is the Lord's command and enough if it is done: tell it as 'it is told'. The poisoned cup and the boiling oil are later legends: note only, or one line marked as legend. Check the Jerome reference and the feast's rank before drafting.
- *(Done: Basil and Gregory and the Two Roads, Athens and Caesarea, 2026-11-19.)* *Basil and Gregory, the two friends at school*  (2 January, General Roman Calendar, memorial of Saints Basil the Great and Gregory Nazianzen; proposed 2026-11-19): after *John the Apostle* the Christmas run of the shelf reaches its first memorial of the new year. Told from Gregory Nazianzen's own funeral oration for Basil (Oration 43, in the Office of Readings for the day): two young men far from home at the schools of Athens who knew only two roads, one to church and one to their teachers, and who shared everything as if one soul had two bodies. Then Basil's hospital and soup kitchen outside Caesarea (the "Basileias", Gregory's "new city", Oration 43.63) and Gregory's reluctance to be made a bishop. Keep the quarrel when Basil made Gregory bishop of tiny Sasima short and fair to both. Check the oration's paragraph numbers, the Office of Readings text and the memorial's rank before drafting; a famine-relief scene must keep to what Gregory says. 'Gregory' already has *Gregory and the Boys in the Market* (Gregory the Great): make the title distinct.
- *(Done: Raymond of Penyafort and the Cloak on the Sea, Barcelona and Majorca, 2026-11-20.)* *Raymond and the cloak on the sea*  (7 January, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial of Saint Raymond of Penyafort; proposed 2026-11-19): after *Basil and Gregory* the shelf's run through the Christmas season reaches the first saint of the new year after the Epiphany. A careful Dominican priest from near Barcelona who was asked by the Pope to sort a great heap of Church laws into one book (the *Decretals* of Gregory IX, 1234), became head of his order and gave it up after two years to go back to being an ordinary friar, and lived, the tradition says, to nearly a hundred. The famous scene is the legend of his crossing from Majorca to Barcelona on his outspread cloak when the king would not let him sail: tell it as a legend, clearly marked, and let the history carry the story (the patient sorting, the giving up of an important job). Check the memorial's rank, his dates (about 1175 to 1275), the *Decretals* year and the tradition that he asked Thomas Aquinas for the *Summa contra Gentiles* (label it tradition) before drafting. 'Raymond' already appears in *Catherine and the Queen of Fontebranda* (Raymond of Capua): make the title and opening name him as Raymond of Penyafort.
- *(Done: Agnes and the Two White Lambs, Rome, 2026-11-20.)* *Agnes and the two white lambs* (21 January, General Roman Calendar, obligatory memorial of Saint Agnes, virgin and martyr; proposed 2026-11-20): the shelf's run through January after *Raymond of Penyafort* reaches the girl martyr of Rome, about twelve years old.
