The shelf has no violence worse than a scolded mouse, so the martyrdom is told in one gentle line ("she would not stop loving Jesus, and she was killed for it, and went to be with him"), and the story's weight goes to what Rome still does: on her feast two lambs are blessed at her church on the Via Nomentana, and their wool is spun and woven by nuns into the pallia the Pope gives new archbishops each June. Before drafting, check: Saint Ambrose's account in *De virginibus* (book 1) and the epitaph of Pope Damasus as the earliest sources; the date of her death (usually put in the persecution of Diocletian, about 304, though some argue earlier); who keeps the lambs and weaves the wool today (the Trappist community at Tre Fontane and the Benedictine nuns of Santa Cecilia in Trastevere have been named; confirm); and mark the later *Passio* details (the hair that covered her, the angel) as legend. 'Agnes' has no Little Lamps or Saints by Candlelight story yet (`overlap.mjs`).
- *(Done: Timothy and the Letters from Paul, Lystra, Thessalonica, Corinth and Ephesus, 2026-11-21; RSV wording; Thessalonica errand ordered by 1 Thessalonians 3 with the Acts 17-18 difference in the note; the handwriting line kept to Galatians 6:11 and 2 Thessalonians 3:17, not the Pastorals; circumcision (Acts 16:3) and Hebrews 13:23 left out; death from the Acts of Timothy in one sentence as legend.)* *Timothy and the letters from Paul* (26 January, General Roman Calendar, memorial of Saints Timothy and Titus, bishops; proposed 2026-11-20): the day after the Conversion of Saint Paul, the shelf's January run reaches Paul's young helper. Told from the New Testament: Timothy of Lystra, whose mother Eunice and grandmother Lois taught him the Scriptures from a child (2 Timothy 1:5; 3:15; Acts 16:1), who travelled with Paul and was sent on errands to the churches, and to whom Paul wrote 'let no one despise your youth' (1 Timothy 4:12). Titus, left in Crete to set things in order (Titus 1:5), can share the story briefly. Keep to what the letters say; that Timothy was bishop of Ephesus is tradition (Eusebius), and the account of his death there is later legend: note only. Note in the grown-ups section that many scholars doubt Paul wrote the Pastoral Letters himself, without arguing it. Check the memorial's rank and the verses before drafting. 'Timothy' has no Little Lamps or Saints by Candlelight story yet.
- *(Done: Simeon and Anna and the Baby in the Temple, Jerusalem, 2026-11-22.)* *Simeon and Anna and the baby in the Temple*  (2 February, General Roman Calendar, Feast of the Presentation of the Lord; proposed 2026-11-21): after *Timothy* the shelf's run through the winter reaches Candlemas. Told from Luke 2:22-40 only: Mary and Joseph bring the forty-day-old Jesus to the Temple with the offering of the poor, a pair of turtledoves or two young pigeons (Luke 2:24, Leviticus 12:8); old Simeon, 'righteous and devout', to whom the Holy Spirit had revealed that he would not die before seeing the Lord's Christ, takes the child in his arms and says the Nunc Dimittis (2:29-32, the prayer the Church says at Night Prayer, which makes it a fit bedtime ending); Anna the prophetess, eighty-four (or a widow eighty-four years: the Greek allows both, say so in the note), who never left the Temple, gives thanks and speaks of him to everyone. Keep Simeon's 'a sword will pierce through your own soul also' (2:35) gentle or in the note. That Simeon was very old is tradition from his words, not stated; the candles of Candlemas are the Church's later custom (the blessing of candles, the 'light for revelation to the Gentiles'), say when and where it is first recorded only after checking (Egeria's account of Jerusalem, late fourth century, is the usual citation: check it). Not a saint's life, so the grown-ups note says Simeon and Anna are honoured in the East and in the Roman Martyrology but the feast is of the Lord. Check the verses and the feast's rank before drafting. 'Simeon' appears in other stories only in passing (`node overlap.mjs Simeon`); there is no Little Lamps story of the Presentation yet.
- *(Done: Cyril and Methodius and the Letters for the Slavs, Constantinople, Moravia and Rome, 2026-11-22; Glagolitic made by Constantine, Cyrillic named for him by later pupils; the rain-sun-air answer as the Life's report, placed at Venice in the note; Methodius's imprisonment in one gentle line; burial place of Methodius unknown.)* *Cyril and Methodius and the letters for the Slavs* (14 February, General Roman Calendar, memorial of Saints Cyril, monk, and Methodius, bishop; co-patrons of Europe; proposed 2026-11-22): after *Simeon and Anna* the shelf's winter run reaches mid-February. Two brothers from Thessalonica are sent to Great Moravia (about 863) because its prince asked for teachers who could speak to his people in their own tongue; Cyril (Constantine) makes an alphabet for the Slavonic language (the Glagolitic script; the later Cyrillic script is named after him but was not his, say so in the note) and they translate the Gospels and the liturgy, so children could hear God's word in the words their mothers used. Keep the opposition (clergy who held that only Hebrew, Greek and Latin were fit for worship) gentle; Cyril dies in Rome in 869, Methodius carries on. Sources to check before drafting: the Slavonic *Lives* of Constantine and of Methodius, John Paul II's encyclical *Slavorum Apostoli* (1985), the *Catholic Encyclopedia* article. Mark legend as legend. 'Cyril' and 'Methodius' appear only in passing in one finished story (`node overlap.mjs --titles Cyril`).
- *(Done: Peter and the Breakfast on the Beach, the Sea of Galilee, 2026-11-22.)* *Peter and the breakfast on the beach*  (22 February, General Roman Calendar, Feast of the Chair of Saint Peter the Apostle; proposed 2026-11-22): after *Cyril and Methodius* the shelf's February run reaches the feast of Peter's charge to care for the Church. Told from John 21:1-19 only: Peter says 'I am going fishing', the disciples catch nothing all night, a man on the shore tells them to cast the net on the right side, the net fills (153 large fish, and it does not tear), John says 'It is the Lord!', Peter jumps into the sea, and Jesus has a charcoal fire with fish and bread ready. Then three times 'Simon, son of John, do you love me?' and three times 'Feed my sheep' (or lambs), the gentle undoing of the three denials (keep those to one line or the note). The feast is of Peter's office, not of a chair as furniture: the note says what the 'chair' (cathedra) means and that the wooden chair in Saint Peter's is a later relic whose dating is debated (check before stating). Check the feast's rank and the verses (RSV) before drafting. *Andrew and the Nets* tells the call by the lake, not this scene: keep the opening different (`node overlap.mjs --titles Peter`).
- *(Done: Polycarp and the Eighty-Six Years, Smyrna, 2026-11-23.)* *Polycarp and the eighty-six years*  (23 February, General Roman Calendar, memorial of Saint Polycarp, bishop and martyr; proposed 2026-11-22): the day after *Peter and the Breakfast on the Beach*, the shelf's February run reaches the old bishop of Smyrna who, tradition says, had known the apostle John. Told from the *Martyrdom of Polycarp* (the letter of the church at Smyrna, among the earliest martyr accounts) and Irenaeus's memory of him (*Against Heresies* 3.3.4, and Irenaeus's letter to Florinus in Eusebius 5.20): the old man who would not run far, who fed the men sent to arrest him and asked for an hour to pray, and who, told to curse Christ, answered (check the wording in a named translation before quoting) that he had served him eighty-six years and been done no wrong. The death must be one gentle line, as *Lucy* and *Lawrence* do; the fire shaped like a sail and the sweet smell are the letter's own wonders and are told as what the letter says; the dove is widely thought a later addition to the text (check and say so in the note). Check the date of his death (often given as 155 or 156; the note says why it is uncertain) and the feast's rank. 'Polycarp' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Cyril of Jerusalem and the Lessons in the Dark, Jerusalem, 2026-11-23.)* *Cyril of Jerusalem and the lessons in the dark*  (18 March, General Roman Calendar, memorial of Saint Cyril of Jerusalem, bishop and doctor of the Church; proposed 2026-11-23): Lent's run reaches the bishop who taught the people preparing for baptism at Easter. Told from his *Catechetical Lectures* (the Procatechesis and the five *Mystagogical Catecheses*, whose authorship some scholars give to his successor John; the note says so) and the pilgrim Egeria's account of Holy Week in Jerusalem (check that she describes the bishop teaching the candidates and the newly baptised in Easter week, and whether her bishop was Cyril): a child among the candidates who is told what baptism means only after it, as the lectures say, and who hears the bishop explain the night of Easter (the turn to the west and to the east, the water, the anointing; check each against the text before telling it). Keep his exiles to one line and take no side in the Arian quarrels beyond what the sources say. Check the feast's rank and that he was declared a doctor of the Church in 1882 (Leo XIII). Keep clear of *Cyril and Methodius and the Letters for the Slavs* (a different Cyril; `node overlap.mjs --titles Cyril`) and of *Simeon and Anna and the Baby in the Temple*, whose note cites Egeria. 'Catechetical' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Turibius and the Catechism in Three Languages, an Andean village, 2026-11-24.)* *Turibius and the catechism in three languages*  (23 March, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial of Saint Turibius of Mogrovejo, bishop; proposed 2026-11-23): Lent's run goes on to the archbishop of Lima (from 1581) who walked and rode his huge diocese for years at a time, learned Quechua to speak to its people, and called the Third Council of Lima (1582 to 1583), whose catechism in Spanish, Quechua and Aymara was printed in Lima in 1584 (check the claim that it was the first book printed in South America, and the printer Antonio Ricardo). A child in an Andean village hears the prayers in her own language for the first time. Check before drafting: the length and number of his visitations, the tradition that he confirmed Rose of Lima (and whether Martin de Porres too), his death at Saña on 23 March 1606 (Gregorian Easter 1606 was 26 March, so that was Holy Thursday; check the sources say so), and what the council decreed about Spaniards' treatment of the Indigenous people, told fairly and without making any group the villain. Keep clear of *Rose and the Hut in the Garden* (`node overlap.mjs --titles Rose`). 'Turibius' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: John Baptist de la Salle and the Lessons in French, Reims, 2026-11-24.)* *John Baptist de la Salle and the lessons in French*  (7 April, General Roman Calendar, memorial of Saint John Baptist de la Salle, priest; proposed 2026-11-24): after *Turibius and the Catechism in Three Languages* (prayers in the people's own language), the canon of Reims who gave up his comfortable place and his family fortune to live with poor schoolmasters and founded the Brothers of the Christian Schools (from about 1680); his schools taught children to read in French first rather than Latin, in classes rather than one at a time, and free. A poor boy in Reims who has never been allowed to stay in school reads his first word. Check before drafting: the dates (founding, the vow of 1691, the Rule), the claim that he taught reading in the vernacular first (and his *Conduct of the Christian Schools*), his giving away his wealth in the famine of 1683 to 1684, the lawsuits brought by the writing masters, his death at Rouen on Good Friday, 7 April 1719 (check the date and that it was Good Friday), and his patronage of teachers (Pius XII, 1950). Keep clear of *Elizabeth Ann and the School for Everyone* (`node overlap.mjs --titles School`). 'John Baptist de la Salle' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Ephrem and the Hymns for the Choir, Edessa, 2026-11-25.)* *Ephrem and the hymns for the choir*  (9 June, General Roman Calendar, optional memorial of Saint Ephrem, deacon and doctor of the Church; proposed 2026-11-24): after *John Baptist de la Salle and the Lessons in French* (teaching children in their own language), the Syriac deacon of Nisibis and later Edessa who taught the faith in hymns sung in his people's own tongue, and who, according to the later poet Jacob of Serugh, trained choirs of women to sing them. A girl in Edessa who cannot read learns a whole hymn by singing it. Check before drafting: his dates (born about 306, died 373 at Edessa; check the 9 June date of death), his move from Nisibis to Edessa after the city was handed to Persia in 363, Jacob of Serugh's *memra* on Ephrem as the source for the women's choirs (and what it says), the title "harp of the Spirit" (check who gave it), his naming as doctor of the Church by Benedict XV in 1920, and the many later Greek writings under his name that are not his (the note says so, and the story quotes nothing from them). Translate any hymn line only from a named published translation. Keep clear of *Hilda and the Cowherd Who Could Not Sing* and *Cecilia and the Song in Her Heart* (`node overlap.mjs --titles Sing Song`). 'Ephrem' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: André and the Door at Notre-Dame College, Montreal, 2026-11-26.)* *André and the door at Notre-Dame College*  (not in the General Roman Calendar: an optional memorial on 6 January in the proper calendar of the United States and on 7 January in Canada's, so the story and note say which countries keep it; proposed 2026-11-25): after *Ephrem and the Hymns for the Choir* (a small person whose gift was not learning), the Holy Cross brother of Montreal who could barely read, was made doorkeeper of Notre-Dame College, and became the man the sick queued to see; his devotion to Saint Joseph grew from a wooden chapel on Mount Royal into Saint Joseph's Oratory. A child who comes to the door every day with a question. Check before drafting: his dates (born 1845 at Saint-Grégoire, Quebec; died 6 January 1937), his admission to the Congregation of Holy Cross despite poor health, the saying usually given as "when I joined this community, the superiors showed me the door, and I remained forty years" (find its source or tell it as "people say"), the oil from the lamp before Saint Joseph's statue and the medals (keep the cures as "people said they were helped", not as fact), the first chapel of 1904, his beatification (1982) and canonisation by Benedict XVI (17 October 2010). Keep clear of *Peter Claver and the Baskets Down the Ladder* (`node overlap.mjs --titles Door`). 'Bessette' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Leo and the King at the River, Rome and the Mincio ford, 2026-11-27.)* *Leo and the king at the river* (10 November, memorial of Saint Leo the Great, pope and doctor of the Church, in the General Roman Calendar; proposed 2026-11-26): after *André and the Door at Notre-Dame College* (a small man who opened a door), a pope who went out of the city to meet someone everyone feared. In 452 Attila's army had crossed into northern Italy, and Leo went with two Roman officials to meet him near the Mincio; Attila turned back. A child in the party who is frightened and walks beside the pope anyway. Check before drafting: Leo's dates (pope 440 to 461; died 10 November 461), the embassy's companions as Prosper of Aquitaine names them (the ex-consul Avienus and the prefect Trygetius), what Prosper actually says happened and what modern historians add (famine and disease in Attila's army, the Eastern emperor's troops), and that the apostles Peter and Paul appearing beside Leo is a later legend (tell it as "an old story says", or leave it out). Keep the meeting free of violence; the army is big and far off, never shown fighting. Also worth a line: his Tome to the Council of Chalcedon (451) and the bishops' "Peter has spoken through Leo" (find its source in the council acts, or omit it). 'Attila' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Bonaventure and the Name at the Bedside, Bosco ai Frati, 2026-11-27.)* *Bonaventure and the name at the bedside*  (15 July, memorial of Saint Bonaventure, bishop and doctor of the Church, in the General Roman Calendar; proposed 2026-11-27): after *Leo and the King at the River* (a brave walk to meet someone feared), a gentle one: a sick little boy in Bagnoregio, and the later Franciscan teacher who wrote the life of Saint Francis and was made a cardinal. Check before drafting: his dates (born about 1217 to 1221 at Bagnoregio as Giovanni di Fidanza; minister general of the Friars Minor from 1257; cardinal-bishop of Albano 1273; died 15 July 1274 during the Second Council of Lyon), that the story of Francis healing him as a child and exclaiming 'O buona ventura' is legend (Bonaventure himself, in the prologue of the *Legenda maior*, says only that he was rescued from death as a child through Francis's intercession, invoked by his mother: quote that, not the legend's words), and the story of the cardinal's hat left hanging on a tree while he washed dishes (later tradition; tell it as 'an old story says' or leave it out). An invented child, perhaps a kitchen boy at the friary, who learns that the great teacher still does small work. 'Bonaventure' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
- *(Done: Romuald and the Little Houses in the Forest, Camaldoli, 2026-11-27.)* *Romuald and the little houses in the forest*  (19 June, optional memorial of Saint Romuald, abbot, in the General Roman Calendar; proposed 2026-11-27): after *Bonaventure and the Name at the Bedside* (a great man at small work), a quiet one about silence: the hermit who built a ring of tiny huts round a chapel in the high woods, each monk with his own little garden. Check before drafting: his dates (born at Ravenna about 950; became a monk at Sant'Apollinare in Classe; died 19 June, traditionally 1027, at Val di Castro; the years are disputed), the founding of Camaldoli in the Casentino about 1023 to 1027, and that the main source is Saint Peter Damian's *Life of Romuald* written about fifteen years after his death. Leave out or soften the duel that sent him to the monastery (his father killed a kinsman; no violence on stage), and treat the later tradition of the dream of a ladder of monks in white going up to heaven as tradition. An invented child, perhaps a woodcutter's daughter who brings bread up the hill and learns to be quiet enough to hear the birds. 'Romuald' is unused in `overlap.mjs`.
